#10450. Fitbit Edge after 30 days: Life in the middle lane [Video]

The biggest compliment you can pay Google at the moment is that alongside the Pixel Watch, recent Fitbit releases have been a huge return to form – the Fitbit Edge included.

I need to nail my colors to the mast; I’m a Fitbit Air fanboy. I’ve talked about it multiple times on the Pixelated podcast, here on the site, and it made me switch away from the Pixel Watch 5 because of how easily it fit into my existence without insisting on itself.

The Fitbit Edge worried me at first because it comes with a screen, which is something I realized isn’t that important to me day-to-day. After 30 days of wear, I’m conflicted because maybe I was too hasty to ditch a display on my wrist.

The design is somewhat in a halfway house because it feels like it’s trying to be both a classic Fitbit while integrating some of the Pixel Watch features we know and love. Initially, I loved it, but it has become something that isn’t quite enough in either direction to suit my own tastes.

My love for the simplicity of the Fitbit Air has colored how I feel, but the Edge has an excellent core design. The display bezels remind me heavily of the original Pixel Watch; yes, they’re there, but you only notice them when the light catches the frame edge.

After a month of daily wear, the display has lost a bit of its luster. The oleophobic coating is just about holding up, but now we’re into the cooler months; it’ll be put through a barrage of jacket cuffs and hoodie sleeves. This is one of the only durability concerns I have had so far.

Despite that admittedly minor concern, the Fitbit Edge is not something you need to baby. I’ve bashed it with weights, caught my wrist on zips (zippers for you, my American friends), and bashed it around with reckless abandon. It’s solid and hasn’t picked up as much as a single scuff. I don’t care too much if a fitness tracker gets a beating, whereas I would be more concerned about damaging my Pixel Watch.

If you come in wanting a smartwatch-like experience from the Fitbit Edge, I think you’ll instantly be frustrated by the UI. It can feel cramped when you see notifications, and the large bezels don’t help all that much, but Google has well optimized almost all of the key sections outside of this.

Tiles are very well implemented; they’re just a smaller, squatter take on the Pixel Watch tile system, and it’s one of the best things about the Fitbit Edge interface. It’s also really snappy, which is something that isn’t the case with cheaper fitness trackers such as this. I’m constantly checking the weather or using the Google Health tile, but I have loved the quick access complication on the default watch face.

I tried a few of the other designs over the past 30 days; a lot of them are great. The default has become my favorite. It’s information-dense without being too overloaded. Tapping the complications takes you to the exact data point. I genuinely love how fuss-free an experience it is, save for a few minor complaints.

We could criticize the accuracy of the data or the lack of tracking for certain types of workouts, but I think that misses the point of the Fitbit Edge. It’s a simple tracker with a screen, and the fact that you get even basic ECG on a device of this size is impressive.

I truly have worn the Edge daily for a full month, and while the bands are comfortable, I have found that they cause me to get a rash on the underside of my left wrist just under the palm. It’s actively stopping me from wearing it all the time now. I hope that the new connector doesn’t slow down third-party development for bands, because I’m desperate for a fabric band to alleviate this issue.

This is exacerbated when the rubber band gets wet or sweaty. If you have sensitive skin, you might have the same problem as I have. Using some moisturizer has helped, but it is something I wasn’t prepared to suffer with before the Fitbit Edge turned up. I get that this is likely a “me” problem, but I wanted to note it.

When the screen gets wet or moist, it can bug out quite a lot. Taps don’t register properly, and a few water droplets can activate all the menus and submenus. Luckily, it won’t force your phone to place a call or anything like that, but the water protection mode is a must. I have kept it on extensively.

Swimmers will probably get super frustrated; heck, I was on a rainy day as the screen kept bugging out. Overall, though, I have enjoyed the fitness tracking. Unlike on the Fitbit Air, it is nice to have a screen to start a workout rather than head over to my phone. That said, the experience is the same as on your phone, but scaled down to fit the watch face.

I dislike that you don’t get a phone notification to show the current workout progress by default, like the Fitbit Air. I had become accustomed to that. Luckily, you can enable it in the Google Health settings. I like having this as I forget to stop workouts so often without a prompt to hit “stop.”

With audio playback, you’re also getting the partial benefits of watch-phone controls, but it’s a two-step process unlike on Pixel Watch. You either have to go to the “Media” app or have the tile active and navigate to it to access play/pause and all your other streaming app controls. I tend not to bother using these as it’s finicky.

This had me thinking about NFC. I think a lot of people would have liked NFC for Google Wallet on the Fitbit Edge, but I think having to enter a PIN on such a small screen would really have been a nightmare. I’ve made wireless payments with my watch less than five times, as it’s just easier to use my phone rather than contort my wrist to a wireless payment terminal. I figure this was something that was thought about and makes a lot of sense given the constraints.

One of my other complaints is that the DND and Bedtime modes from my account haven’t synced or at least don’t appear to sync. Unless I’m mistaken, you need to activate these settings manually every time, which has been super annoying when in bed, as a flick of your wrist can blind you in a dark room. I figure this is something that can be fixed with an update, so here’s hoping.

I’ve been super impressed with the battery life. A week is easily attainable for me with up to three gym visits per week, so I’ve charged 5 times in a month. That, to me, is excellent. I picked up a cheap charging dock, as the Edge uses the same charger for the Fitbit Air, so I figured why not.

It’s not the best-looking dock, but I quite like how it airs off my Edge so it’s not as stinky after gym sessions – as I wipe with an alcohol pad and clean as regularly as possible.

The experience is fine; it’s almost a third of the price of the Pixel Watch and lacks a few features due to the various compromises – compromises I can deal with, as smartwatches have become information overload for me over time, and I like that the app selection is ultra-refined. At no point does the Fitbit Edge give you information overload.

Coming from a Charge 6 or an older Fitbit, this is an amazing replacement. It’s the new “default” fitness tracker for people who want all the hallmarks of what makes a great Fitbit.

There is just enough. Nothing more, nothing less. To me, this feels like the perfect middle ground for a lot of people. You want something simple, touchscreen-enabled, and that doesn’t get in the way. As impressed as I have been with the Edge, though, I’ve loved a fuss-free tech accessory so much recently that I’ll probably go back to the Fitbit Air.

#10447. Vivo X500 Pro camera review: Battle of the small(er) phones vs Pixel 11 Pro

The compact Vivo X500 Pro has some camera downgrades compared to the Pro Max, so are you better off saving money and getting the base Vivo X500 instead?

Over the past few years, Vivo has made a name for itself as the camera powerhouse smartphone company. The first time I used a Vivo X100 Pro blew my mind in a way smartphone cameras hadn’t for a few years, but does the company’s 4th iteration — yes, Vivo skipped the X400 — of the X series still impress?

The answer is mostly yes. The Vivo X500 series includes five models: the base X500, the X500 Pro, and the X500 Pro Max. Unlike some other phones, all three of these models feature different camera hardware that takes advantage of the physical size differences. The X500 Pro Max’s hardware is the best of the bunch, but that phone is substantially larger and heavier as a result.

So I’m comparing the more reasonably sized models to see which semi-compact Vivo phone you should consider this year. I tossed the Google Pixel 11 Pro into the comparison since Google also offers a compact flagship camera phone.

Most camera specs between these phones are surprisingly comparable, so the differences you’re about to see are largely caused by some pretty massive differences in processing. I already covered Vivo’s wild new AI camera mode, so let’s go for something a little closer to normal smartphone camera use: a day at the Biltmore.

Perhaps the biggest difference between these phones is Vivo’s pursuit of “perfection,” while Google seems to embrace a more “natural” look. At 10x and 5x, the photos from both Vivo phones are essentially noise-free, have clear sharpening applied to them, and are generally quite vibrant. Despite having similar specs, the Vivo X500 Pro’s more advanced Sony LYT telephoto sensor pulls out more detail.

Google’s images are notably softer, although not in a low-resolution sort of way. They look more like they’re taken from a standard camera rather than a smartphone, which some people may prefer. While telephoto image quality between the Vivo X500 Pro and Google Pixel 11 Pro is mostly down to preference, Google’s phone is substantially faster while taking the actual photo. In all of the darker shots, the Pixel 11 Pro took the picture instantly. Both Vivo X500 models took a 2-3 second exposure shot, making me stand there for longer than I’m used to.

Ultrawide performance is where I feel we see the largest gap between these phones. Both the Vivo X500 and X500 Pro cameras offer bright, well-balanced shots that are vibrant but not unrealistic. The light sources in the room emit a lovely warm glow, yet the bulbs themselves aren’t overblown. The X500 Pro’s slightly warmer image across all three scenarios looks best to me.

The Pixel 11 Pro’s ultrawide images are often underexposed and lacking in dynamic range. The staircase shot exhibits this problem the most. This can come off as natural in some scenarios, as modern smartphone photography is notorious for brightening dark areas too much, but too often the Pixel 11 Pro’s ultrawide shots just feel unnaturally dark.

The main camera is much more balanced, although it’s fun to push these phones to find their weaknesses. One of the best examples of differing priorities is in the wreath shot, which was quite dark in real life thanks to the bright sunlight outside. The Pixel 11 Pro valiantly tried to brighten up the shadows so you could actually see the wreath (since it’s clearly the subject), causing the rest of the scene to look forcefully bright.

The same issue cropped up in the image of the wine rack, where both Vivo phones captured a nicer-looking shot. The image of the trellis walkway is a tie, but the photo of my ridiculous bowl of ice cream looks best on the Vivo X500 Pro, once again.

Portrait mode is where I think the Vivo X500 Pro is the clear loser, but it’s not for any of the reasons in the other categories above. Rather, the X500 Pro seems to have some weird warping happening in the bokeh processing that’s not present on the base Vivo X500. This is in both front-facing and rear camera portrait shots, so it’s not a lens issue or some weird setting.

Google cleaned up its portrait shots so much this year that it’s borderline ridiculous. Pixels had the worst portrait mode for years, but this year’s overhaul of the portrait algorithm made all the difference in the world.

Overall, the Vivo X500 series is another solid release for Vivo, and while neither the base nor Pro models offer the same top-tier camera specs as the Pro Max model, it’s clear Vivo pulled off something great. The Vivo X500 Pro retails for €1,399 in the EU, while the base X500 global model is a little harder to find, but should be quite a bit cheaper if you can get your hands on it.

#10445. Google Play wants Android apps to nail foldable basics by April 2027

Google Play is introducing new adaptive app and game quality requirements next year to elevate the Android foldable experience.

Starting April 2027, the Play Store will require developers to meet Tier 3 of the Android adaptive app quality guidelines for foldables.

Referred to as “Adaptive ready” or “basic” support, Tier 3 requires apps not to use letterboxing (or other compatibility modes) and to fill the entire screen. During rotation, resizing, and folding/unfolding, apps must preserve scroll position, text input, and media playback. Apps must also support multi-window environments.

Apps that do not meet this quality bar will no longer be able to publish or update on Google Play, and may have reduced visibility on the store.

At the same time, Google Play will require foldable device screenshots for app listings: “Providing foldable screenshots by April 2027 will become a standard submission requirement when publishing new apps or updating your store listing.”

Curious to see whether the requirements will actually be as strict as they make them seem (on paper).

If so, then also Google will have a ton of work to do to modernise all of their apps.

By August 2027, new and updated apps will be required to target Android 17 (API level 37).

Over time, this “quality framework will progressively expand to incorporate Tier 2 and Tier 1 standards across foldables, tablets, Googlebooks, and Android XR.”

Apps that meet Tier 2 (better) or Tier 1 (best) will get “premier store badging, editorial spotlighting, and enhanced merchandising across Google Play as the platform bar elevates.”

On the gaming front, titles will need to add foldable screenshots to listings by April 2027, while Tier 3 large screen quality guidelines go into effect in August 2027.

#10440. Markdown to PDF in 2026: No-Install Methods and the Rendering Problems Nobody Warns You About

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Converting Markdown to PDF is easy until the document has real content: long code blocks, wide tables, images and non-Latin text are where most tools fall apart.

Pandoc with LaTeX is still the most powerful route, but it needs a large install and trips over Unicode and emoji unless you switch engines and fonts.

Browser-based approaches (print CSS, Chromium-powered converters, in-browser tools) avoid the install and give you HTML-quality rendering, as long as you control page breaks.

A handful of print CSS rules (@page, break-inside: avoid, break-after: avoid) fix the majority of ugly PDFs, whichever tool you use.

Choose the tool by the document: a quick README export, a long technical report and a multilingual document each have a different best option.

I write almost everything in Markdown: READMEs, release notes, client handovers, meeting notes. And at least once a week, someone asks for "the PDF version". Every time, the conversion looks fine on the first page and falls apart somewhere around page three: a code block split in half, a table running off the edge, an emoji replaced by an empty box.

SitePoint covered the classic route back in 2015 with Creating PDFs from Markdown with Pandoc and LaTeX. That approach still works. But a lot has changed since then, and today there are several ways to get a clean PDF without installing a TeX distribution. This article compares them, then goes through the rendering problems you'll actually hit, with a fix for each.

Pandoc is still the Swiss Army knife. The simplest command is:

By default, that goes through LaTeX, which means you need a TeX distribution installed (several gigabytes for a full one). The output is beautiful for prose and math, but LaTeX has strong opinions about layout, and customizing it means writing LaTeX.

Pandoc can also render through an HTML engine instead, which lets you style the PDF with CSS:

Best for: long documents, academic writing, math, automated pipelines.

Watch out for: install size, Unicode handling with the default engine (more on that below).

Render the Markdown to HTML (any previewer, a static site, a GitHub page) and print it to PDF. It's free and already installed everywhere, but you inherit whatever the page looks like on screen: navigation bars, sidebars, and page breaks wherever they happen to fall.

Best for: quick, one-off exports of short documents.

Watch out for: zero control over page breaks unless the page ships print CSS.

Extensions like Markdown PDF for VS Code, or CLI tools like md-to-pdf, render your Markdown to HTML and print it with a headless Chromium:

You get the same rendering quality as a browser, plus a config file for CSS, margins, headers and footers. The trade-off is a Node install and a downloaded Chromium.

Best for: developers who live in their editor or want CI-friendly exports.

Watch out for: the first run downloads a browser, and print CSS is still your job.

The newest option is tools that do the whole conversion inside the browser tab: you paste or drop the .md file, preview the actual pages and download the PDF. The good ones don't upload your file anywhere, which matters for client documents and internal notes.

Best for: people who don't want to install anything, non-developers on your team, private documents.

Watch out for: many online converters do upload your file to a server; check before you paste anything confidential.

Whichever method you pick, the same handful of problems show up as soon as the document is longer than a page. Most of them are fixed with print CSS, which works in Pandoc's HTML engines, Chromium-based tools and any HTML you print from a browser.

A 20-line function cut after line 3, with the rest on the next page, is the most common complaint. The fix is to ask the renderer not to break inside the block:

One caveat: a block longer than a full page has to break. For those, it's better to let it break cleanly than to push it to a new page and leave a huge gap.

On screen, a long line in a code block scrolls horizontally. On paper, there's no scrollbar, so it's simply cut off. Wrap long lines in print:

Tables with many columns overflow the page or get squeezed until every cell is one word per line. Three things help: a smaller font size for tables, a landscape page for the worst offenders, and avoiding breaks in the middle of short tables:

If a table genuinely needs eight columns, consider whether it should be a table in the PDF at all, or an appendix in landscape.

A heading alone at the bottom of a page, with its content on the next one, looks broken. This rule keeps a heading attached to what follows it:

Support for break-after: avoid varies between rendering engines, so test it with yours; when it isn't honored, a manual page break before the heading is the reliable fallback.

Markdown has no syntax for a page break. The usual trick is a raw HTML element that print CSS turns into a break:

Some converters also recognize a comment like , which has the advantage of staying invisible when the same file is rendered on GitHub.

Images are inserted at their natural size, so a 4000-pixel screenshot spills off the page. Cap them:

For blurry images, the problem is usually the opposite: a small screenshot stretched to page width. Export screenshots at 2x if they're going into a PDF.

This one is specific to the LaTeX route. With the default pdflatex engine, Unicode characters outside its supported set produce errors or simply vanish. Switch to XeLaTeX or LuaLaTeX and give it a font that actually contains the characters:

Emoji are harder still in LaTeX and often need a dedicated package. HTML-based renderers handle both far better, because they use the operating system's font fallback just like a web page does.

Internal links ([see setup](#setup)) and external links should stay clickable. HTML-based renderers usually keep both. If yours doesn't, check that headings get IDs (most Markdown parsers add them) and that the PDF engine preserves link annotations.

Put together, these rules make a solid starting point for any HTML-based route:

Save it as print.css and pass it to Pandoc (--css=print.css with an HTML engine), to md-to-pdf through its config, or include it in the HTML you print from the browser.

My rule of thumb after a lot of trial and error:

A quick README or notes export, no install, private content: an in-browser converter. Full disclosure: this need is why I built the free Markdown to PDF converter on AwesomeToolkit, and it's what I use for this case now. It renders real A4 or Letter pages as you type, has an option to keep code blocks and tables whole, adds page numbers and a table of contents, and the conversion happens in the browser, so the file isn't uploaded anywhere.

Documentation in a repo, exported in CI: md-to-pdf or Pandoc with an HTML engine, plus the stylesheet above.

Long reports, math, academic formatting: Pandoc with XeLaTeX, using a font that covers every script in the document.

Something you'll print once and forget: the browser's Print to PDF is fine.

Whatever you choose, the biggest quality jump comes from the print rules, not the tool. Ten lines of CSS turn "it looks fine until page three" into a PDF you can actually send.

Yes. Your browser's Print to PDF works on any rendered Markdown, and in-browser converters do the whole conversion in a tab. Check that the converter processes files locally if the content is private.

The default pdflatex engine has limited Unicode support. Use --pdf-engine=xelatex or lualatex and set a font that contains the characters with -V mainfont= (and -V CJKmainfont= for CJK text).

Markdown has no native syntax for it. Insert

where you want the break, or use the page-break comment your converter supports.

Add pre { break-inside: avoid; } to your print CSS. Blocks longer than a page will still break, which is expected.

With HTML-based renderers, usually yes, both external links and links to headings inside the document. Test internal links in particular, since they depend on headings having IDs.

#10439. Review: Amazon’s Alexa Tablet 8 is a Fire Tablet with Google Play for $150 – what’s not to love?

Amazon shocked everyone in October when it launched its new Alexa Tablet series with Google Play support built-in, addressing the biggest shortcoming of its older Fire Tablets. For Black Friday 2026, the Alexa Tablet 8 is a mere $150 and, with Google Play on board, this feels like a Fire Tablet that’s all grown up.

The Alexa Tablet 8 is one of three new Amazon tablets that launched in October with Google Play on board, and it’s also the one I was personally the most excited about. I love a good tiny Android tablet, but there just aren’t that many out there – and some of the ones we do have are exorbitantly expensive.

Right up there with Amazon’s long-running reputation in the tablet space, Alexa Tablet 8 is very much an affordable device, and that’s reflected in its fairly meager specs:

Yet, I didn’t find myself put off by the device.

I specifically asked Amazon for a review unit of the absolute base model – 4GB of RAM and lockscreen ads – and I can say with confidence it’s Not Bad at the end of the day.

The display is objectively not good, but it’s not really trying to be flashy either. The panel gets bright enough for most indoor use and, for general browsing or watching videos around the house or even on a flight, it gets the job done. If anything, my biggest complaint on the display was just how smudgey it gets with fingerprints.

Hardware is a strong suit. The Alexa Tablet 8 has an aluminum build that comes in this gorgeous teal color as well as a softer “Rosette” pink (or “Graphite,” if you want to keep it boring). It’s thin and light and slips into a bag easily, and even fit into the back pocket of my jeans. Buttons are clicky and aspects such as the speakers actually do well enough – like the display, the speakers aren’t good, but they’re not bad by any stretch either.

Amazon has a folio cover available that’s simple but nice, with a fabric finish that’s grippy. It’s an extra $40, though, which feels like a bit much for a super-affordable Android slate.

The biggest question was around the software experience, and this is where this new effort really shines.

Alexa Tablet 8 ships with Android 16 out of the box, not FireOS or whatever Amazon was calling its forked experience on Fire Tablets (I genuinely refuse to go double-check because it’s a memory I’m thrilled to never have to revisit). It’s a pretty “stock” experience, with Amazon leaving aspects such as the notification shade/quick settings alone, and even the Settings menu feels pretty clean.

That’s not to say nothing has changed. The homescreen has a fairly standard layout , but with these neat little corner widgets – one a “dynamic” widget with scrolling content (mostly ads) and the other a shortcut for Alexa search. Amazon is pushing Alexa as a voice assistant pretty heavily on these tablets, but I’m happy to report it’s pretty easy to ignore if you choose. These tablets will be great additions to your Alexa ecosystem if you’re invested, but I’m simply not.

The biggest change you’ll find is the lockscreen, where Amazon’s base models all come with lockscreen ads. This saves you about $20 on the hardware, and it’s not that bad of an experience. That said, I’d absolutely spend the extra $20 at purchase to go without them, especially seeing as this blocks the ability to set a lockscreen wallpaper at all. It doesn’t cost anything more to remove them later, though, with a $20 charge through your Amazon account removing ads.

But what’s important here is that, for the fire time, Amazon is admitting defeat and installing the Google Play Store.

Alexa Tablet 8, and the rest of the lineup, has Google Play out of the box and access to any app you want. This means that you can, if you choose, fully ignore Amazon’s own services and do whatever you want. The smallest of examples: While Amazon’s built-in keyboard is fine, it’s wonderful that I don’t have to jump through hoops to switch over to Gboard. Fire Tablets were always an enticing value with a big catch, but Amazon’s change in this regard truly makes Alexa Tablets an attractive option for everyone – not just those willing to put up with the restriction or put in the work to hack Google apps on board.

Performance, despite the meager specs, is not awful. MediaTek’s low-end chip has just enough horsepower for general use, and 4GB of RAM isn’t as detrimental as I would have expected. It’s not fast by any means, but you could do a lot worse. There are 4GB Android tablets I’ve literally decided not to review because I couldn’t use them past the setup process, so the fact that Amazon is delivering a completely usable experience is a win in my book, even more so that I don’t actually mind using it. There’s a caveat to that, of course, and it’s that you shouldn’t expect to do a ton on this base model. For light browsing, reading Kindle books (or whatever other reading app since, you know, Google Play), or watching videos, this is great. For games, you’ll want to look elsewhere (and expect to spend more).

Alexa Tablet 8 isn’t some shining example of a perfect budget tablet, but I think it really nails the fundamentals. At its usual $230~ starting price, it’s a plenty-good deal for those in the market for an affordable Android slate, but the Black Friday pricing of just $150 is all the better – I just wish the model with 8GB of RAM was also on sale.

Amazon’s pivot to Alexa Tablets is great – though I hope the company knows full well that everyone is still going to call these Fire Tablets or Kindles or whatever combination of brands that spring to mind. But that reputation might work in Amazon’s favor at the end of the day. While there’s a huge portion of the audience that’s been left frustrated for years at the lack of Play Store support on Fire Tablets, they’ve still been a market success for the most part, so this shift is just a win for everyone. Users get a better experience, Amazon gets to shuffle its priorities away from a dead app store, and the market as a whole gets an affordable piece of hardware that doesn’t suck.

#10436. Google Pixel officially re-releases App Lock in beta, Samsung Galaxy gets it too

It’s been a wild ride for Google Pixel’s App Lock feature, but the company is officially re-releasing the feature today, right alongside its debut on Samsung Galaxy devices.

The feature is still only in Android 17 QPR3 Beta 3 for Google Pixel devices, an update that should be released to the stable track in early 2027.

Pixel owners on stable Android 17 QPR2 should already have this! Why would Google make Pixel owners wait until March 2027 to get this?! Release it now to Pixel owners. 🤨

You can still access App Lock by long-pressing on an app icon and adding it to the app lock list.

In a post on Reddit, Google’s Community Engagement Manager details that App Lock on Google Pixel was removed for improvements before getting a full release:

…the product team decided to temporarily pull back the feature from the QPR2 Beta to allow for additional polish, thorough testing, and robust integration across all system surfaces.

That’s why Samsung’s One UI 9.5 beta release today also brings support for App Lock. The feature is built into Android 17 QPR2, seemingly the release that One UI 9.5 is based on, but Google Pixel devices aren’t getting it until QPR3. As far as release timelines go, this means that Samsung’s Galaxy S27 series will probably ship with this feature out of the box, where Pixel phones won’t get it until around March or so. One UI 9.5 won’t roll out for older Galaxy devices until May, if One UI 8.5 serves as an example.

#10434. Motorola Razr Fold finally gets its Android 17 update – hopefully the next 7 years go faster

Motorola has kicked off its Android 17 rollout for the Razr Fold, marking the first major update for the company’s first book-style foldable, and the start of a potentially interest 7 years of software support.

The Motorola Razr Fold launched with Android 16 back in May 2026, and it remains a really good option for those looking to pick up a book-style foldable in the US. On top of solid specs across the board, it also carries the promise of 7 years of software support, something that few other Moto devices can tout.

But nowhere was it promises that Motorola would speed things up at all. A little over 3 months after Motorola announced the update and several weeks after other Motorola devices were updated to Android 17, the Motorola Razr Fold is finally getting its own Android 17 update.

Rolling out now, Android 17 for the Razr Fold is a 4.15GB~ update that appears to be available at least somewhat broadly. Reports are popping up on Reddit including one report from the EU (Hungary, specifically), with another user apparently in a Spanish-speaking region also seeing the update.

We’re still waiting to see if the update is available in the US (there’s a backlog of updates on our unit), but it’s certainly an update to look forward to. Apparently, Motorola finally fixed a weird UI issue that affected the Telegram app, something we ran into when using the device earlier this year.

It’s great to see the update out, but it’s noteworthy that Motorola is not setting a particularly encouraging precedent here. Razr Fold is one of the company’s first devices to be promised 7 years of OS updates and software support, and its flagship-tier status certainly would have most expecting it to be updated ahead of some of Motorola’s more affordable, lower-tier devices that were updated over a month ago. Whatever the case, hopefully Motorola will speed things up over the next few years, especially with its $1,600 flagship Signature 27 on the horizon with the same software promises.

#10431. New gradient icons roll out to Google Messages & Contacts

Google this week completed the rollout of its new gradient icons with updates for Messages and Contacts.

The icon shapes remain the same for all three communication apps, though they are slightly larger and better fill the circular backgrounds. Contacts is still a head and body, while Messages remains overlapping chat bubbles and Phone a handset. This redesign is not particularly disruptive.

A lighter shade of blue is leveraged, while all feature a gradient effect. This results in the logos feeling less heavy and a bit more modern.

Google describes the new Messages icon as “sharper, crisper, and clearer than ever before.” The SMS/RCS client did not roll out any other changes with the logo redesign this week. The big homepage redesign is still in the beta channel.

Google Contacts was the last Android icon to be refreshed, with version 4.92.26.x rolling out on Wednesday evening. The Phone by Google update rolled out on Monday to stable users.

It took two weeks from announcement to full availability across the three apps.

#10429. Calling in sick: Vice City

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Not sure if you’ve heard, but the biggest video game of all time is out today: buying Anthropic stock. Yes, in case you haven’t already blown all of your money on the GTA VI Ultimate Edition, you can try your luck at buying one of the most anticipated IPOs of all time when ANT goes public on the Nasdaq later today.

But you’re likely up, playing the game that is, according to Metacritic, already among the best of all time. It’s currently sitting at a near-perfect 99/100 on the aggregator, with reviewers calling it “a masterpiece” and “gaming’s… greatest love story.” It’s a game in which you can do basically anything you want, from the violent to the vacuous.

More than anything, though, I’m just excited to discover all the different ways the game allows you to find and consume coffee.

As I’m sure happened in every newsroom, the conversation this week turned to whether traffic will meaningfully decline today as up to 1.5 million people “call in sick” to play GTA VI. If you’ve been awake since the crack of dawn trying to meander your way to Vice City, you’re just one of many folks experiencing the rarest of moments in 2026: a singular, unifying cultural event.

My biggest question isn’t whether gamers will play GTA VI but whether normies will. Previous games in the series, particularly GTA V, sold hundreds of millions of copies and still draw countless players today. GTA VI feels even more inviting to those who just want to spend time in its sandbox to do some fishing, dancing, or flying. One reviewer even called it a “cozy game” where he just spent time walking around a zoo.

While the size, scale, and impact of GTA VI are exponentially higher, the last time I heard people talking about a game in this way was during the pandemic, when everyone was hunkering down for a winter of Animal Crossing.

By the way, GTA VI has a functioning smartphone that can be used for making calls, sending texts, taking photos, looking at “Instagram,” and setting reminders, among other things. It’s yet another minor detail in a game full of them. Plus, it looks like an original Pixel.

Usually with sponsored content, I have to spend the whole time convincing you that the product is worth buying. But RedPocket’s Black Friday deals do all that work on their own.

Not only do you get amazing discounts for the first 12 months of service by prepaying for a full year, but RedPocket’s “Lock-in Low” promise means that you get to stay as close to that promotional price as possible once the first year is over.

There’s no contract and no port-in requirement. You can keep your number, keep your phone, and activate with an eSIM on compatible devices. During the Black Friday sale, there’s even a Refer-a-Friend credit that gets you $25 for every person you bring to the service.

Explore RedPocket’s plans or compare the annual offers. This is one of the best Black Friday decisions you’re going to make this month, and maybe ever.

Google has finally rolled out its answer to Grok Bot, ChatGPT Dots, and Claude Duet, and it’s called… Tasks? Spark has transitioned to Tasks, and the features are rolling out in six countries for all free Gemini users. I’ll have more to say about this next week.

Also interesting is that Google has released yet another AI model, called Neon, that essentially fits between its brand new Carbon model (which isn’t yet widely available, and which ostensibly replaces Argon) and Hydrogen. Confused yet? Don’t be. It’s easy.

If older (and freer) games are more to your taste, someone ported Unreal Tournament 2004 to the browser, where I just spent a few minutes reliving some of my favorite gaming moments of my youth.

These charts showing Nvidia’s growth over the past few years are astounding, enraging, and a little scary. Those are big numbers.

On this week’s Sideload, we hosted Eric Migicovsky of Pebble fame to ask him about his newest products, the Pebble Time 2 and Pebble Round 2, along with his feelings on AI and the current state of the tech industry.

Finally, at this year’s DealBook Summit, Andrew Ross Sorkin interviewed a cavalcade of famous people, including Sam Altman, Elon Musk, Zohran Mamdani, David Ellison, and Mark Carney.

#10425. This portable monitor is great for DroidDeck, and now it’s down to $85

I hooked it up to an Anker Prime Power Bank (on sale for 30% off right now).

#10422. What Is the Best UI design Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com.

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

BackstopJS is a free, open-source visual regression testing tool released under the MIT license. You list the URLs, viewports, and CSS selectors to capture in a config file, and BackstopJS renders them in a headless browser through Playwright or Puppeteer, then compares the results against approved reference screenshots. The core workflow runs on three commands: backstop init, backstop test, and backstop approve.

Strengths: Completely free, with no screenshot limits. A built-in browser report shows reference and test images side by side with a visual diff inspector, and changes can be approved from there. Optional Docker rendering keeps screenshots consistent between laptops and CI; Playwright adds Firefox and WebKit, and click, hover, and keypress selectors capture interactive states. JUnit reports plug into existing CI pipelines.

Trade-offs: BackstopJS is self-hosted, so there's no cloud dashboard, shared team review, or pull request status checks out of the box. Reference screenshots are usually committed to your repository, and every page or component state you want to test needs its own URL and scenario in the config. Someone on the team also has to own the setup, CI integration, and maintenance.

Best for: Developers and small teams with no budget who are comfortable running and maintaining their own visual regression setup.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, self-host BackstopJS, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic or Percy.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, For visual testing, Chromatic and Percy both offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month, and BackstopJS is free to self-host.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

Corsair's AIR 5400 RS-R midtower PC case is down to its lowest price ever, with a 31% discount bringing the price down to $169.99. The case normally retails for nearly $250, and outside of a short sale last month, we haven't seen it drop below $200. In addition, with a wrap-around glass side panel and triple-chamber design, the case comes with three Corsair RS-R 120mm PWM ARGB fans, which normally run $60 on their own.

Typical of these multi-chamber case designs, the AIR 5400 RS-R supports most hardware you can throw at it. You can use anything from a mini ITX motherboard to an E-ATX board, and it has mounting points for up to two 360mm radiators, plus an additional 120mm mounting point. The 360mm worth of mounting space on the bottom of the case is occupied by the pre-installed fans and an airflow shroud.

Additionally, the case is compatible with rear-connector motherboards from Asus, MSI, and Gigabyte, so you can have a cable-free front chamber. The case doesn't have a vertical GPU mount, but there's ample room for even larger graphics cards, with a maximum length of 430 mm supported. Like most multi-chamber cases, this one is primarily designed around liquid cooling. However, you have a generous 180mm of CPU cooler clearance if you decide to go with an air cooler.

The Corsair AIR 5400 RS-R is a midtower PC case with three Corsair RS-R 120mm ARGB fans pre-installed. It supports up to E-ATX motherboards, as well as rear-connector boards from MSI, Asus, and Gigabyte.

The Corsair AIR 1500 RS-R is loaded with enough features to justify its $250 list price, let alone its current sale price of $170. In addition to wide cooling and GPU compatibility, along with rear-connector motherboard support, the case comes with a built-in anti-sag arm to support your GPU, the height of which you can adjust depending on your GPU. Be warned, however, that the final fan on especially long GPUs may hit the support. Thankfully, you can remove it.

Corsair includes its RapidRoute 2.0 mounting system, giving you a pegboard-style motherboard tray that provides mounting points virtually anywhere for cable management. Cable managing multi-chamber cases is much easier than a traditional tower, as well, with ample room behind the motherboard tray to hide your cable mess.

Around the front, the curved-glass side panel shows off the internals of your rig from the side and front. To get inside, the curved panel splits apart like French doors, so you can service your machine without juggling a few different pieces of tempered glass. The rear side panel is on a hinge for easy access, as well.

The one major downside with this chassis is that it doesn't come with any front panel USB-A ports. Instead, you get three USB-C ports, split between two USB 3.2 Gen 1 ports and a single Gen 2 port, alongside a 3.5mm headphone/microphone combo jack. Given that most motherboards have one or maybe two USB-C ports, extra front panel connectors are always nice to have. However, even a single USB-A would go a long way.

#10417. Are the Galaxy Buds 4 Pro the best earbuds for you?

Picking the right earbuds is a tough task, but Samsung’s Galaxy Buds 4 Pro are among the best on the market, and in this guide, we’ll be looking at whether or not they’re the best earbuds for you this holiday season.

In short, yes, the Galaxy Buds 4 Pro sound great. Samsung’s flagship earbuds have a balanced sound profile that’s customizable by the listener to suit their tastes, and is generally recognized as having excellent sound quality by experts and owners alike.

Galaxy Buds 4 Pro are award-winning earbuds with balanced sound quality for music lovers, offering a clean and crisp profile that lets you hear music the way you’ll enjoy it most.

Samsung’s flagship earbuds deliver their sound through a new, larger main driver that’s paired with a dedicated tweeter. They support 24-bit/96 kHz UHQ audio when paired with a compatible Samsung Galaxy device. That’s paired with active noise cancellation that adjust to your surroundings in real-time. They also sound good for those on the other end of a call, with Samsung’s Super Clear Call tech reducing noise from your environment, and the blade stem design positioning the microphones closer to your mouth.

Like everything else in Samsung’s product lineup, Galaxy Buds 4 Pro are packed with useful features.

This includes active noise cancellation (ANC) to filter out noise in your surroundings. Samsung even adapts ANC strength to what’s around you, raising the ANC level when you’re somewhere like on a plane, but lowering it when you’re in a quiet park or café. Voice and Siren Detect can automatically switch the earbuds to Ambient mode when you begin a conversation or when an emergency siren is detected.

Live Translate offers support for 22 languages to help you have a conversation beyond the language barrier, with speech translated into your ears via Buds 4 Pro and the transcript shown on your phone. Bixby and Gemini are accessible through the earbuds for AI assistance, too. Samsung also offers an FDA-approved hearing aid feature to help amplify sounds around you.

Head gestures allow you to answer or decline calls with a simple shake of your head. Pinch controls manage playback, while swiping adjusts the volume; pinch-and-hold can be customized for supported shortcuts.

No, you don’t have to have a Samsung Galaxy smartphone to use Galaxy Buds 4 Pro.

Because they work over standard Bluetooth, Samsung’s best earbuds work with any smartphone, tablet, and other devices. Galaxy Buds 4 Pro can also connect to Windows PCs as standard Bluetooth audio, although advanced features are more limited outside the Galaxy ecosystem.

That said, you’ll get the best experience with Samsung devices. That’s in part because EQ, noise cancellation, and other controls are baked into One UI, but also because Samsung’s best audio codec on Galaxy Buds 4 Pro is proprietary to Galaxy devices. In other words, you’ll get the best sound quality on these earbuds when paired to another Samsung device.

Galaxy Buds 4 Pro are typically sold for $249.99, but it’s not hard to find a good deal, especially during the holiday season. Best Buy is going to be one of the best resources here, with discounts available throughout the holiday season alongside convenient in-store pickup.

That's a significant performance loss, but the Core i7-14700K still manages to match the gaming performance of the Ryzen 7 5800X3D, which is otherwise the fastest DDR4 gaming CPU on the market.

The Core i7-14700K slots into existing LGA 1700 motherboards, though you'll need a Z-series chipset if you want to manually overclock the chip. Although Intel has moved on from the LGA 1700 socket, reports suggest the company is working on new 'Raptor Lake Next' chips that will use the same socket.

#10408. Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

Long-context training — including pretraining and post-training, has become a foundation of frontier AI models. Longer native sequence lengths give large language models (LLMs) the working memory to read a whole code repository or ingest hours of video, and reason through multi-step agentic tasks. In three years, context windows have grown from 128K–200K tokens to 1M–2M, and models trained toward 10M tokens are arriving. Training at those lengths, not just serving them, is where the system limits show up.

In particular, efficient training at multi-million-token sequence lengths runs into the following bottlenecks:

Today, we are excited to announce long-context training support in MaxText, Google Cloud's open-source JAX training framework. MaxText enables this by supporting four context-parallel strategies on Cloud TPU: All-gather, A2A (DeepSpeed Ulysses), P2P (Ring Attention), and the A2A + P2P hybrid (Unified Sequence Parallelism, or USP), combined with load balancing and sequence packing. All four use the Tokamax Splash Attention kernel. Combined with the 3D inter-chip interconnect (ICI) ( supported on TPUs such as TPU 5p or TPU 7 (Ironwood) ), this lets one sequence span an entire slice while communication overlaps compute and memory per chip stays flat. In our benchmarks, Llama 3.1 8B (25 head dim, 16 query heads) demonstrates exceptional long-context efficiency, achieving 70% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) on TPU 5p for sequence lengths up to 4 million tokens. Across both TPU 5p and TPU 7, context can be seamlessly extended to 14 million tokens—with TPU 7 consistently maintaining 50% MFU across all sequence scales below 6 million tokens.

This functionality is available today in MaxText v0.2.4(doc).

Context parallelism splits a long sequence across chips, so no chip holds the activations of the whole sequence. Context parallelism can be combined with data and tensor parallelism, and the smallest context-parallel size that fits your sequence in HBM is usually the right one. This also holds for sparser attention variants which cut attention compute but not the activations of every other layer.

Attention is the one global operation where every token needs the keys and values of every preceding token, so each context parallel strategy has to bring those keys and values back together. Each makes a different tradeoff between memory (how much KV a chip holds), communication (how much moves per layer, and whether it overlaps compute), and model shape (how many attention heads to split).

All-gather: Each chip keeps its chunk of the sequence and, at every layer, gathers the keys and values from every other chip. It is the simplest option. However, because causal masking causes later tokens to attend to larger prefixes, All-gather requires balancing compute load across devices.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

All-to-All (Ulysses): Gathering keys and values makes every chip hold the whole sequence, so attention memory never shrinks no matter how many chips you add. All-to-All changes what each chip owns instead. Attention is independent across heads but not across tokens, so the head axis is free to shard and the token axis is not. An All-to-All before attention rotates the sharding from one onto the other: each chip gives up the heads it is not responsible for and receives the tokens it was missing for the heads it keeps, ending up with the full sequence for a few heads instead of a few tokens for all heads. This is also why Ulysses does not need token load balancing. Attention then runs as ordinary single-chip attention with standard kernels, and a second All-to-All rotates the output back. KV per chip shrinks by the context-parallel factor, but you cannot split across more chips than the model has heads — and tensor parallelism shards that same dimension, so TP × CP is capped by the head count (under GQA, by the KV heads: eight for Llama 3 8B). The all-to-all itself, though, does not overlap with compute — attention cannot start on a partial sequence, so every chip waits for data from every other chip before any of them begins.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

P2P (ring attention): Ulysses' ceiling is the head count; ring attention removes it by never leaving the token axis. Keys and values never materialize in full. KV chunks travel around a ring of chips one hop at a time, neighbor to neighbor. Each chip attends its queries to the chunk it holds while the next one is in flight, and merges the partial results as it goes. A chip holds its own chunk and one visiting chunk, never the full sequence, and the communication overlaps compute. However, dividing attention computation into smaller chunks can reduce local kernel efficiency, and unlike All-to-All Ulysses, ring attention requires explicit load balancing.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

A2A + P2P (USP): A2A + P2P (USP): Neither strategy scales on its own — All-to-All stops at the head count, and a long ring accumulates hops while thinning the overlap that makes it work. Unified sequence parallelism composes them on two axes of the chip mesh, so the context-parallel degree becomes the product of the two: All-to-All along one axis, sized at or below the KV-head count, and a ring along the other to extend past it. Because each All-to-All group has already pooled its tokens, a ring step now carries a chunk larger by the All-to-All factor, and the ring is correspondingly shorter — more compute per hop to hide the transfer behind, and fewer hops to accumulate latency and causal imbalance. On Cloud TPU the two map onto two axes of the slice's chip mesh.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

These strategies cover the attention variants in today's major models, from multi-head and grouped-query attention to DeepSeek-style multi-head latent attention.

Splitting the sequence only pays off if the attention compute on each chip stays efficient. All four strategies run on Splash Attention, the block-sparse flash attention kernel from Tokamax, Google's open-source library of TPU kernels. It skips fully masked blocks and handles packed sequences without padding. P2P calls it once per ring step, on the local queries and the KV chunk that has just arrived, while the transfer for the next step runs underneath, in the forward and backward pass alike. Each chip does the same fused, block-level work it would do without context parallelism, with the communication hidden behind it.

P2P (Ring Attention) is the primary workhorse for very long-context training, whereas All-Gather is the standard baseline for most other cases where the KV can fit. These are the primary CP strategies to use in practice. We compare how far each can push context length on a fixed 64-chip TPU 5p slice with Llama 3.1 8B (256 head dim, 16 query heads) , and measure their MFUs. In ideal settings where both strategies can have large enough chunks (S/CP) and local batch size - the two major variables driving MFU on TPU 5p, we observe that P2P can outperform All-Gather almost every context length from 64K to 14M tokens, where communication becomes mostly hidden with the Ring circular transport schedule.

TPU v7x (Ironwood) takes the same recipe further. With twice the HBM per chip, P2P training of 14M tokens on 64 TPU 7 chips reached 48.7% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) compared to 37.1% on TPU 5p. We observed that ring attention performs better than other context parallelism strategies -- e.g. Ulysses and USP –- on TPU 7 chips.

The choice of context parallelism strategy depends mostly on whether the gathered KV fits in HBM, and on how many KV heads the model has:

For all CP strategies, when you run low on memory: Offload before you recompute. MaxText can move saved activations, optimizer states, and parameters to host memory tensor by tensor. At long context lengths, these transfers are hidden behind compute, making offloading effectively free. Offload attention outputs first, followed by optimizer states, layer inputs, parameters, and finally remat as a last resort. MFU stays flat through the offload steps while all-gather falls off and eventually stops fitting. For more details, see the remat tuning guide.

Looking ahead, we see two natural extensions. The first is broader model coverage. Models that mix sliding-window and global layers, such as Gemma, would run the window layers with a short exchange between neighboring chips while the global layers use the ring. We also plan to support linear attention mechanisms such as Gated Delta Net (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). The second is performance, with further tuning of the Tokamax kernels and the P2P backward pass to narrow the remaining gap to all-gather at short context, and recipes by model and context length for TPU 5p and 7 chips.

Context parallelism is available today in MaxText v0.2.4 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Sample recipes are available here for TPU 7 and TPU 5p. Pick a strategy with context_parallel_strategy (ring, ulysses, or usp), set the context-parallel size, and keep the rest of your run the same. The sharding guide has the flags and constraints for each strategy, and the release notes link to the pull requests behind this work. We can't wait to see what you train.

#10403. Build Live Video Chat With WebRTC: From First Call to Production Debugging

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Adding real-time video to a web app used to mean plugins, proprietary SDKs and a lot of money. Today every modern browser ships with WebRTC, a set of APIs that lets two browsers exchange audio, video and data directly. The catch is that WebRTC gives you building blocks, not a finished feature. This guide walks through those blocks in the order you actually need them, with the exact API calls you need, and then covers the production issues that tutorials usually skip.

A browser-to-browser call has four moving parts:

Start with the local preview. Browsers only allow camera access on HTTPS or localhost, so serve your page accordingly.

1.     Request a stream with navigator.mediaDevices.getUserMedia(), passing video constraints (an ideal width of 1280 and height of 720 is a sensible default) and audio constraints with echoCancellation and noiseSuppression enabled.

2.     Assign the returned MediaStream to the srcObject property of your local video element.

3.     Keep a reference to the stream, because you will add its tracks to the peer connection later.

Set the muted and playsinline attributes on the local video element. Without muted users hear themselves, and without playsinline iOS Safari opens the video full screen.

The peers need a way to find each other. A tiny Node.js server using the ws package is enough for development. Its whole job is to relay messages between the peers in a room:

1.     Create a WebSocket server (for example with the ws package) listening on its own port.

2.     Keep a map of room names to the set of sockets in each room.

3.     When a client sends a join message, store the room name on the socket and add it to that room's set.

4.     For every other message type, forward the raw message to the other sockets in the same room, and never echo it back to the sender.

5.     On disconnect, remove the socket from its room so stale peers do not receive messages.

In production you would add authentication, limit room size to two for one-to-one calls, and validate message types, but the shape stays the same.

Both sides create an RTCPeerConnection, add their local tracks and listen for the remote ones:

1.     Create a new RTCPeerConnection with an iceServers list that contains at least one STUN server.

2.     Loop over the local stream's tracks and call addTrack() for each one.

3.     In the ontrack handler, attach the first remote stream to your remote video element.

4.     In the onicecandidate handler, send every new candidate to the other peer through your signalling channel as a candidate message.

One peer creates an offer, the other answers, and both apply each other's descriptions. Candidates arrive asynchronously in the background.

1.     The caller runs createOffer(), applies it with setLocalDescription() and sends the description as an offer message.

2.     The callee applies the offer with setRemoteDescription(), runs createAnswer(), applies it locally and sends it back as an answer.

3.     The caller applies the answer with setRemoteDescription().

4.     Both sides pass every incoming candidate message to addIceCandidate().

If both peers might start a call at the same time, look up the "perfect negotiation" pattern on MDN. It assigns one side as polite and resolves offer collisions cleanly instead of leaving both peers stuck.

On a home network, STUN alone often works. On corporate Wi-Fi, mobile carriers and symmetric NATs it frequently does not, and the call silently fails to connect. A TURN server relays media when a direct path is impossible. The open-source coturn server is the usual choice.

In practice, your iceServers list should contain your own STUN URL plus a TURN URL with a username and credential, so the browser can fall back to the relay automatically when direct connectivity fails.

Generate short-lived TURN credentials on your backend rather than hard-coding them, and budget for bandwidth: relayed calls pass every frame through your server.

Users deny camera access, unplug headsets and switch from Wi-Fi to 4G mid-call. Catch NotAllowedError and show a clear explanation, listen for devicechange events, and use RTCRtpSender.replaceTrack() to swap cameras without renegotiating the whole call.

Listen to pc.onconnectionstatechange. When the state becomes disconnected, wait a few seconds before giving up, because it often recovers. On failed, call pc.restartIce() to try new network paths.

Use RTCRtpSender.setParameters() to cap the bitrate on weak connections, and read pc.getStats() to monitor packet loss and round-trip time. Showing a small "poor connection" indicator does more for perceived quality than any codec tweak.

Mesh calls, where every participant connects to every other, work for two or three people. Beyond that, upload bandwidth explodes. Group calls need a Selective Forwarding Unit (SFU) such as mediasoup, LiveKit or Janus, which receives each stream once and forwards it to the others.

Tutorials end at a working demo, but the fastest way to understand what production WebRTC looks like is to watch a real call from the inside. Chrome ships an internal diagnostics page that shows every peer connection the browser opens, and it works on any site that uses WebRTC.

1.     Open chrome://webrtc-internals in one tab and keep it open.

2.     In another tab, start a call on your own demo and find its RTCPeerConnection entry.

3.     Expand that entry and look at the ICE candidate pairs: you will see whether the call went direct (host or srflx) or through a TURN relay (relay).

4.     Open the stats graphs for the outbound video stream and watch the bitrate, frame rate and resolution adapt when you throttle the network in DevTools.

5.     Check the negotiated codec and how quickly the connection recovers when you switch Wi-Fi off and back on.

Once you can read your own call, repeat the exercise on a production service. Any browser-based product works, whether a meeting tool or a live video chat that pairs two visitors, because webrtc-internals shows the same data for every site. Expect differences from your demo: more candidate pairs, a relay candidate in the list and a bitrate that adapts faster. Seeing them makes the earlier sections concrete, from why TURN matters to how bandwidth estimation reacts to packet loss.

The one-to-one call above is the core of many products, from telehealth consultations and tutoring to customer support. The engineering challenges grow with the use case: random matching needs queueing and presence on the signalling server, public platforms need moderation and reporting, and global audiences need TURN servers in several regions.

Start with the minimal version, get it working across Chrome, Firefox and Safari on both desktop and mobile, and add TURN before real users arrive. Once the call is reliable, every other feature becomes a much easier problem.

#10393. Dev squeezes an AI language model onto a floppy for 486DX and Pentium PC stalwarts

If you are still running on a 486SX maybe it is time to upgrade.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An inspired developer has shoehorned a language model onto a floppy disk created especially for 486DX and Pentium PC users running DOS. Please note that WollyMonjac’s project isn’t a large language model (LLM). Though it shares the transformer model architecture of its much bigger siblings, Tiny 2.1 is humbly described as “a tiny question-answering transformer written in Turbo C 2.0.”

There isn’t much room for modern software on a floppy disk, with a typical PC formatted disk in the 486 era boasting just shy of 1,475,000 bytes of data capacity. Tiny 2.1 makes the best use of this limited headroom as possible, fitting in the following:

Your creaky old PC will need about 540KB of free conventional memory to run Tiny 2.1. If you do test it, we are sure you will appreciate the clean UI with its conversation history pane, neural monitor panel, and Ask Tiny prompt window. As all DOS programs should, there’s a range of F-key shortcuts highlighted within the UI. Perhaps the retro-futuristic cherry on the cake, though, is the PC-speaker robot voice which can be used to speak the responses.

We’ve already indicated that the dev seems rather humble about this DOS program. The GitHub repository says it was “Written by Fable 5.2 and trained by Opus 5.5.” Monjac also wryly admits that outside of this language model’s training, “it makes things up confidently.” We have heard less about LLM hallucinations in recent months, but here it feels almost like a fun feature.

Should any of Tiny 2.1’s knowledge gaps irk you, WollyMonjac has included a training folder in the MIT-licensed distribution. A dedicated user could probably build up a capable question-answer transformer tailored to their favorite topic(s).

Nowadays it is pretty easy to get old software to run on your shiny new hardware, one way or another. It isn’t entirely a one-way street, though; getting modern apps or games to run on hardware from decades earlier is an exciting development trend we are seeing established. In addition to the headlining Tiny 2.1, we’ve reported on similarly incongruous new-meets-old software projects like the ChatGPT client for DOS, or Minecraft running on a Game Boy, creating images on the C64 with generative AI, or playing Doom on an Atari ST.

Hopping around the electricity grid, recharging on power lines, NOMADs don’t need to come home.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Tech startup Nomadic showcased its NOMAD drones this week alongside an announcement that it had raised $6 million in seed funding. These quadcopter drones have a unique skill hinted at by their name: they can basically fly forever without returning to base. NOMADs recharge by perching on high-voltage power lines, a ubiquitous feature of most landscapes, before moving on to their next task.

We hadn’t heard anything about the NOMAD drones until now, but Nomadic says it has tested its quadcopters for over 20,000 hours on live lines in Germany, Norway, and the U.S. “NOMADs are drones that land on live high-voltage lines, charge from the line itself, and relocate across the grid to where measurements are needed,” summarizes the startup.

The reference to measurements along the power grid reveals the current purpose of NOMAD drones. They are deployed to monitor the high-voltage lines from which they leech their energy. This is a very useful task for power companies. Nomadic explains that while modern grid operators use weather models to decide how much power each line can carry, the qualities of the lines can be quite variable. Meanwhile, the weakest point in any line “sets the limit for the whole line,” says the startup.

Fixed sensors have started to become the accepted solution for line monitoring, but mobile drones are a tantalizing alternative. In comparison to fixed sensors, the NOMAD, which recharges inductively from the line itself, seems like an ideal solution. Normally, an investigative high-voltage power line check with a human operator would also require extensive planning, heavy machinery, and the line to lose power for a short service period. Nomadic claims that a NOMAD drone can look after around 25km of line, while fixed sensors are typically positioned from 1km to 5km apart.

In addition to the drone hardware, there’s some integrated software to make the best use of the highly configurable data you can harvest from your drone fleet. “Nomadic OS combines their data with weather and grid models, giving operators a real-time picture of what is physically happening on their lines.”

NOMAD drones are also surprisingly tough. Units have been tested in temperatures as low as minus 25 degrees Celsius, in strong winds, and on lines up to 420kV (European transmission grid typical maximum). With the new funding, Nomadic is hoping to expand its fleet and build with more partners. The startup is also looking for several new recruits, mostly in engineering roles, all based in Munich, Germany.
