#10386. AI features could be turned off with a simple toggle, Canva proves

AI has taken over everything in the past few years, with AI features bombarding virtually every app or service available. If you’ve ever wanted to just turn off AI features, Canva proves that it could be a simple toggle, if companies actually wanted to give you a choice.

Canva today announced “AI Controls,” a new toggle that completely turns off generative AI features within the editing app.

Generative AI can be quite helpful in working on posters, editing images, and more, but it’s not everyone’s preference. Canva puts this well:

For some people, AI is the most exciting creative tool in a generation, while others prefer bringing their ideas to life without it. We believe the future of creativity should give everyone the freedom to choose their own path, whether that’s creating with AI, without it, or somewhere in between.

To give its users the choice, Canva’s Permissions menu now has a simple toggle for “Enable Generative AI features.” Toggling it off disables features such as Magic Write and Magic Edit. There’s also an option to limit how much AI-generated imagery appears in the Canva library.

AI has its place, but everyone should have a choice like this, especially when it comes to apps and services used for artistry in the same vein as Canva. Yet, the tech industry continues to go in the opposite direction, shoving AI into every corner whether it’s actually useful or not. Canva proves, though, that it doesn’t have to be that way.

#10384. Proton Pass is up to 60% off for Black Friday—and it does way more than just store your passwords

There’s a familiar interruption that comes with shopping online: you’ve found what you want, you’re ready to check out, and the store asks you to sign in. Was this the account with your usual password? Did you change it last time? Is it easier to reset it again?

Proton Pass is designed to take that friction out of your day. It creates strong, unique passwords, remembers them for you, and fills them in when you log in. But it also handles something a password alone can’t: keeping your real email address out of another retailer’s database.

For Black Friday, Proton Pass Plus and Pass Family are both 60% off their regular monthly pricing with a one-year subscription, while Proton Unlimited is 50% off. Pass Plus starts at $1.99 per month, billed annually, making this a good opportunity to get your passwords — and a few other important parts of your digital life — organized.

The problem with strong passwords isn’t understanding why you need them; it’s keeping track of a different one for every account.

Proton Pass does that work for you. When you sign up for a website, its password generator can create a strong password that’s completely unique. Save it in your vault, and Proton Pass can fill in your credentials when you return.

That’s useful whether you’re creating an account for a single purchase or signing into a service you use every day.

Unique passwords also help limit the fallout from a data breach. If a retailer exposes your login credentials, using a different password elsewhere means those same credentials won’t unlock your other accounts.

With unlimited logins and encrypted vaults that sync across devices, Proton Pass lets you keep those details together as you move between your phone and computer.

Proton Pass is available for practically every platform, from Chrome and Firefox on the web to native macOS, Windows, and Linux apps to, of course, iOS and Android. This makes it trivially easy to access your passwords, passkeys, and API credentials wherever you find yourself.

Black Friday shopping often leaves something behind long after the packages arrive: a steady stream of promotional emails from stores you barely remember visiting.

Proton Pass includes unlimited hide-my-email aliases, giving you an alternative to handing out your real address every time you create an account.

An alias is an extra email address that forwards messages to your normal inbox. You can give it to a shopping website and still receive your order confirmations and delivery updates, without sharing the underlying address you use for everything else.

If that alias starts receiving spam, you can switch it off. Your real email address stays the same, and you don’t have to update all your other accounts.

It’s a practical way to put some distance between your primary inbox and the websites you’re trying for the first time. A separate alias for each service also gives you more control: you can stop messages arriving through one address without disrupting the others.

Passwords are only part of what’s worth keeping in a secure, accessible place.

Proton Pass also stores notes, credit card details, and other confidential information in end-to-end encrypted vaults. That makes it useful for the small, everyday moments when you need something sensitive and don’t want to go looking for it.

When you’re booking a flight, saved passport details let you look up the information you need without digging through a drawer halfway through the reservation.

When you’re shopping online, saved card details mean you don’t have to leave the checkout page to find your wallet.

When you’re traveling, keeping a digital copy of an important document within reach gives you a convenient reference while you’re away from home. It doesn’t replace an original document where one is required, but it can save you from searching through scattered files and messages.

Pass Plus also includes a built-in two-factor authenticator, Dark Web Monitoring, and secure link and vault sharing. Those features give you more ways to manage access to your accounts and share sensitive information when you need to.

Pass Plus is the straightforward choice for one person who wants password generation, autofill, unlimited aliases, and secure storage.

Pass Family includes six Pass Plus accounts and a family admin panel. If several people in your household need a password manager, it’s worth considering rather than setting everyone up with separate subscriptions.

Proton Unlimited bundles Pass with Proton VPN, Proton Mail, Proton Drive, and more, including 500 GB of cloud storage. It’s the option to consider if you also want a VPN, encrypted email, and cloud storage alongside your password manager.

A password manager isn’t the most conspicuous Black Friday purchase, but it’s one you can put to work immediately: the next account you create, the next password you forget, or the next time a checkout asks for details you don’t have handy.

Proton’s offers are available to new users and include a 30-day money-back guarantee.

#10383. Building Email Integrations: APIs, Webhooks, Automation, and Deliverability

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Email remains an important communication channel for web applications. From account verification and password resets to product announcements and customer notifications, many applications depend on email to keep users informed.

For developers, however, implementing email functionality involves more than connecting a service and sending a message. A reliable email system needs to handle authentication, dynamic content, delivery failures, user preferences, event tracking, and integrations with other application components.

Using an email service provider (ESP) can simplify this process. Most modern providers offer REST APIs, SMTP relays, templates, automation features, and reporting tools that developers can integrate into existing applications.

This guide explores the technical considerations involved in building email integrations, including API-based sending, webhook handling, automation workflows, and deliverability.

There are two common ways to connect an application to an email delivery service: SMTP and REST APIs.

SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) is a standard protocol used to transfer email messages between servers. Developers can connect an application to an SMTP server using credentials and configuration settings.

REST APIs provide another approach. Instead of communicating through an email server protocol, an application sends HTTP requests to an endpoint. The provider processes the request and returns a response.

Both methods can support transactional and application-generated emails, but their implementation differs.

An application already supports SMTP configuration.

Developers want a relatively straightforward integration.

Existing frameworks or libraries provide built-in SMTP support.

An application needs programmatic control over email operations.

Developers want structured responses and error handling.

Email functionality needs to integrate with other application services.

The system requires event tracking or additional API capabilities.

The right approach depends on the application's architecture, expected email volume, and integration requirements.

An API integration allows an application to trigger emails in response to specific events.

For example, an ecommerce application might send an order confirmation after a successful payment. A SaaS application could send an account verification message after registration.

A typical integration follows this process:

The backend prepares the recipient and message data.

The backend authenticates with the email provider.

The application records the response and handles any errors.

Consider a Node.js application that needs to trigger an email campaign through an API.

Some platforms, including Mailmodo, provide REST API functionality for triggering campaigns and integrating external applications with email workflows.

The following example demonstrates the general structure of an HTTP request using a campaign-triggering endpoint:

This example uses the documented Mailmodo trigger endpoint and request structure. The campaign ID and API key must be replaced with the values from the relevant account.

Notice that the API key is loaded from an environment variable rather than written directly into the source code.

This is an important security practice. Credentials should never be exposed in frontend JavaScript, public repositories, or client-side application bundles.

Not every email provider offers the same integration capabilities. Developers should evaluate providers based on their API documentation, authentication methods, supported endpoints, event notifications, and integration flexibility.

For example, Elastic Email provides a REST API that developers can use to send emails and manage different email operations from an application.

Its API uses HTTPS requests and API-key authentication. Developers can use the available endpoints to send messages, manage campaigns, retrieve information, and configure webhook notifications.

When evaluating an email API, consider these technical requirements:

Authentication: Check how API keys are generated, stored, restricted, and rotated.

Documentation: Look for clear endpoint descriptions, request examples, response formats, and error codes.

SDK availability: Official libraries can reduce the amount of integration code required.

Permission management: Separate credentials for sending emails and accessing account information can reduce unnecessary exposure.

API limitations: Review connection limits, request timeouts, rate limits, and account-level restrictions before deploying an integration.

A provider with extensive features is not necessarily the best option for every project. The important consideration is whether its API supports the application's actual requirements.

Sending an email is only one part of the communication process. Applications may also need to know what happens after a message is submitted.

A webhook allows an external service to send an HTTP notification to an application when a particular event occurs.

Depending on the provider, events may include:

For example, an application could receive a delivery-failure notification and update the corresponding email record in its database.

A basic Express.js webhook endpoint might look like this:

This is a simplified example rather than a complete provider-specific webhook implementation. A production endpoint should validate the provider's signature or other authentication mechanism and follow its documented payload structure.

It should also handle duplicate events safely. Webhook providers may retry notifications when an application does not respond successfully.

An idempotent event handler prevents the same notification from creating duplicate database updates or triggering repeated actions.

Email automation connects application events with predefined communication sequences.

Instead of manually sending every message, developers can create workflows that respond to user activity.

Consider a SaaS application with the following registration process:

If the user does not complete onboarding, a follow-up message may be triggered.

A simplified workflow can be represented as:

In a production application, email delivery should generally be handled through a background job queue rather than blocking the registration request.

A queue also makes it easier to retry temporary failures, manage sending volume, and prevent email delays from affecting the main application.

Developers should consider tools such as Redis-backed queues or framework-specific job processors when building larger systems.

Automation platforms can also help manage more complex journeys. For instance, a developer can connect application events to an external email workflow instead of implementing every communication sequence independently.

The key is to keep business logic in the application while using the email service to manage message delivery and relevant campaign operations.

A successful API response does not necessarily mean that an email has reached the recipient's inbox.

Email deliverability depends on several technical factors, including domain authentication, sender reputation, message quality, and recipient engagement.

Developers should pay attention to the following configurations.

Sender Policy Framework identifies the mail servers authorized to send messages on behalf of a domain.

A correctly configured SPF record helps receiving servers evaluate whether an email originates from an authorized source.

DomainKeys Identified Mail adds a cryptographic signature to outgoing messages. Receiving servers can use the corresponding public key in DNS to verify the signature.

Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance builds on SPF and DKIM. It allows domain owners to publish policies for handling messages that fail authentication checks and receive reports about authentication activity.

These three mechanisms should be configured carefully, especially when an application sends emails through multiple providers.

Email systems generate useful operational data. However, developers should avoid treating every available metric as an accurate measure of user engagement.

More reliable application-level signals can include:

These events can be connected with email campaign data to understand how communication affects application activity.

Developers should also maintain a clear separation between email delivery records and user account records. This makes it easier to investigate delivery issues without unnecessarily modifying customer data.

Before deploying an email integration, developers should review the following areas:

API security: Store credentials in environment variables or a dedicated secrets manager.

Domain authentication: Configure SPF, DKIM, and DMARC.

Error handling: Account for failed requests, invalid recipients, and temporary provider errors.

Background processing: Use queues for asynchronous email delivery.

Webhook validation: Verify incoming notifications and handle duplicate events.

Monitoring: Track delivery failures, bounce rates, and application-level outcomes.

Testing: Test email templates across different clients and devices before production deployment.

These practices help create an email system that is easier to maintain, troubleshoot, and scale.

Email integration is an important part of modern application development, but a reliable implementation requires more than connecting an SMTP server or calling an API endpoint.

Developers need to consider authentication, asynchronous processing, webhook security, automation logic, domain configuration, and event monitoring.

REST APIs are useful for applications that need programmatic control over email operations, while SMTP remains a practical option for systems that already support standard mail-server connections. Webhooks add another layer by allowing applications to respond to delivery events and update their internal records.

By choosing an integration method that fits the application architecture and following sound security and deliverability practices, developers can build email functionality that supports both immediate communication needs and long-term application growth.

#10382. On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

Creditors want to ensure they could still sell the AI chips in case SpaceX defaults on its loan.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elon Musk is borrowing $43 billion to finance SpaceX's acquisition of Nvidia AI chips for its ambitious data center projects and is using the semiconductors as collateral. However, Ars Technica reports that lenders are concerned that SpaceX will use the chips for its planned orbital data center, making it difficult, if not impossible, for creditors to reclaim in case the company defaults on its loan. SpaceX said it will deploy the collateralized chips only on Earth, but it remains unclear whether this provision is included in the signed debt instrument.

SpaceX is working on building massive data centers on both Earth and in space. Musk has already revealed the company’s first-generation AI1 satellite, with a 120-kilowatt payload and a span that’s larger than a Boeing 747, while he’s also planning to build a $24-billion gas-powered AI supercomputer in Starbase, Texas. These projects are in addition to the Colossus 1 and Colossus 2 sites near the Tennessee-Mississippi border.

Despite being one of the richest people on Earth right now, Musk is relying on a financing deal to acquire the chips he needs to achieve his dream of building an AI LLM that will “assist in the pursuit of understanding.” The $43 billion “Project Saturn” loan is also not structured like a typical loan, so its investment-grade rating would remain unaffected. Instead, it’s built similarly to aircraft leasing agreements, in which a leasing company buys the jets and leases them to the airline that wants to use them. This saves the airline from having to put up a significant amount of cash to get the airplanes that it needs.

For this deal, the Saturn Funding Trust, a fully owned subsidiary of asset-backed lending company WHCO, would acquire the Nvidia GPUs for SpaceX, with the latter paying the former quarterly for the privilege of using the AI chips. The deal is also described as a “five-year triple-net, hell-or-high-water finance lease arrangement,” meaning SpaceX must pay the lease for five years, whether the chips break, become obsolete, or the project becomes unprofitable. It’s also responsible for all the expenses of operating the chips.

These terms are very lender-friendly, as SpaceX basically assumes all the risks while also keeping them off its books. Rating agency Moody’s also said in its report that the chips will be deployed in SpaceX’s U.S. data centers. Nevertheless, the company’s plan to blast data centers into space is making some lenders nervous, regardless of its Earth-bound plans and promises.

#10377. Which Waterdrop water filters are actually worth buying this Black Friday?

Black Friday can turn something as simple as a home water upgrade into a real headache. Do you need a system that fills a glass in seconds, or one that keeps always-ready hot water  available right on the counter? Maybe you’re more worried about finding one that leaves enough room in the budget for the rest of your holiday shopping. Whatever your hydration demands might be, there’s an easy answer at a great price: Waterdrop Filter.

Waterdrop Filter’s Black Friday & Cyber Monday deals run from November 19th through November 30th, making this month the best time to make a meaningful upgrade to your everyday water.

This year, Waterdrop has everything from premium reverse osmosis (RO) water filtration for a house full of holiday guests to budget-friendly, installation-free setups for cozy apartment evenings. And the best part? No matter which you pick, you’re on your way to better-tasting water for drinking, cooking, and those extra demands that come with holiday hosting — complete with some exclusive 9to5Google discounts.

If your kitchen transforms into your home’s central hangout spot whenever guests are around, the Waterdrop X16 Reverse Osmosis System makes a strong case for going premium. Its 1,600-gallon-per-day rated capacity is the highest of the under-sink models here, perfect for a kitchen where glasses, pitchers, pots, and mugs are filled constantly throughout the day.

The X16 RO System is as powerful as that capacity makes it sound. It features Waterdrop’s excellent 11-stage reverse osmosis filter, which traps and removes debris, contaminants, and everything else you don’t want in your water, while its alkaline remineralization process restores that much-needed calcium and magnesium your body needs. Its compact, tankless design tucks neatly under your kitchen sink, while a digital faucet shows water and filter information and lets you select the exact amount of water you want to dispense.

The X16 also features IAPMO certification to NSF/ANSI 42 and 58 for specified performance claims, plus NSF/ANSI 372 for lead content in the parts that contact water. You don’t just have to take its word, though: Wired called the Waterdrop Filter X16 the “Rolls-Royce of water filtration.” The cleanest, clearest water available, ready for your refreshment every day of the year.

The X16 usually costs $1,999, but Black Friday pricing knocks 35% off to bring it down to $1,299. This year, you can use promo code 9TO5GBF26 to take another 5% off the sale price, bringing your final cost down to $1,234. Those are some incredible savings over the X16’s typical list price, and considering its faster flow and dispensing controls over competing models, it’s one of those rare Black Friday purchases you’ll feel great about for years to come.

Under-sink reverse osmosis doesn’t have to break the bank. For a family that wants a smart faucet and plenty of filtered water ready for cooking, coffee, and everyday drinking, the Waterdrop G3P800 Reverse Osmosis System is absolutely the model for you to consider this Black Friday.

The G3P800 RO System features an 800-gallon-per-day rating, 10-stage filtration, UV treatment, a tankless design, and a 3:1 pure-to-drain ratio. Those are well beyond basic specs for water filtration, but at a price you can feel good about. And since the G3P800 is certified to NSF/ANSI 42, 53, 58, and 372 standards, you can rest assured that every sip features reduced chlorine, lead, PFOA, and PFOS.

Waterdrop has the G3P800 RO System marked down 30% for Black Friday, bringing the price to just $699, but 9to5Google readers can use code 9TO5GBF26 to another 5% off for a grand total of just $664.

If you’re just getting started with reverse osmosis water filtration — or maybe you’re looking to introduce your friends or family to the benefits of cleaner hydration — the Waterdrop G5P500A Reverse Osmosis System might be the best fit this holiday season. It keeps the tankless setup from the G3P800, combining it with a smaller 500-gallon-per-day rated capacity, eight-stage filtration, and alkaline remineralization. While you do lose out on the G3P800’s smart faucet and UV treatment, the price point here simply cannot be beat.

At $190 — down 23% from its $259.99 MSRP, plus 9to5Google’s additional 5% discount — the G5P500A RO System gives you under-sink reverse osmosis without making a premium faucet part of the purchase. After all, the best water filter is the one with the features you’ll actually put to good use, and for a smaller household, this model still goes well beyond the everyday basics.

If under-sink installation isn’t an option for your home, Waterdrop Filter has two very different answers, both new to its lineup in 2026. Your choice comes down to whether you want hot and cold water ready and on-demand, or a pitcher you can keep filled in the fridge for perfectly chilled water whenever you want it.

The Waterdrop M6CG Mineral Hot & Cold Reverse Osmosis System dispenses mineralized water from 59°F to 203°F. That means that, from sunup to sundown, morning coffee to that midnight glass of water, you’re drinking clean, crisp, filtered water. Plus, separate clean-water and wastewater tanks and a 3:1 pure-to-drain ratio prevent backflow pollution and extend your filter’s lifespan.

If you’re someone who’ll use hot and cold water regularly, the Waterdrop M6CG RO System is a no-brainer. You can pick it up for just $390 after promo code 9TO5GBF26, way below its typical $539 list price.

If you don’t need the temperature controls, the Waterdrop C2SL Mineral Reverse Osmosis System keeps things simpler and costs much less. A Waterdrop reverse osmosis system can make sense at any of these price points. The X16 is for the household that will put its speed and controls to work. The G3P800 keeps smart features at a lower price. The G5P500A makes under-sink RO more affordable than ever. And for those with limited space, countertop models let you decide how much hot-and-cold convenience you want without ever touching your home’s plumbing system. Whichever fits your routine, don’t wait — Waterdrop Filter’s Black Friday and Cyber Monday offers run through November 30th.

#10373. At $600, the Pixel 10 is this year’s no-brainer Black Friday smartphone deal

Phones have gotten a lot more expensive over the last year or so, and it’s a trend we’re expecting to get worse before it can get better. If you’re in the market for an upgrade, this year’s Black Friday might be one of the final truly good times to buy a new device, and for just $600, last year’s Pixel 10 should be high on your list.

While buying year-old hardware typically comes with some pretty deep sacrifices between generations, that’s not particularly true with the Pixel 10 this year. From a hardware perspective, nearly everything is either identical or close enough to not particularly matter. Its design and display are all but unchanged, Qi2-ready magnets come on both models, and both devices are built with the same level of battery longevity in mind. And much to the chagrin of many a Pixel die-hard, Tensor G5 and Tensor G6 are practically interchangeable.

So what are you giving up that matters? When the Pixel 11 arrived, software features made up the bulk of this list, but with last week’s Pixel Drop, a lot of the gaps between these two generations have been closed. Gemini Intelligence with Rambler, app automation, and more are in the process of rolling out to existing Pixel 10 users, and new features like Create My Widget arrived on the Pixel 10 and 11 at the exact same time. From a software perspective — Google’s bread and butter, in my eyes — the only big sacrifice comes from the year of software support that’s already complete, leaving you with just six instead of seven.

Realistically, the camera is the one place you’re likely to feel a difference. The Pixel 10 was the first non-Pro device in Google’s Tensor era to ship with a telephoto lens, but it came with some big sacrifices. The main sensor is effectively an A-series lens, and taken together, the entire package might leave something to be desired. It’s far from a bad camera though — I used it as my main shooter through most of its lifecycle — and while the Pixel 11 did get some upgrades here, I think real shutterbugs would be better off looking at Google’s Pro lineup anyway. You’ll also lose out on things like HiLight, though really, are you missing that much?

What makes this deal so good is really its place in the market, not as a direct comparison to the newer Pixel 11. For $600, Google’s competition is going to have a hard time competing with the value the Pixel 10 delivers. Hell, even Google might find it difficult to compete in the coming months; at full price, $600 is what the Pixel 10a goes for now, a decidedly less-impressive device compared to this model. No, this is a flagship smartphone through and through, and for $600, you’re effectively guaranteeing a reliable, powerful smartphone experience from now until 2032 — hopefully enough time for this whole RAM crisis to finally simmer down a bit.

#10371. Honor just supercharged its smartwatch lineup with a new Pro model

The Honor Watch 6 Pro has a 5,500-nit OLED display, ceramic bezel, 18-day battery life, and costs less than it looks.

In the world of athletic smartwatches, it isn’t often that you could mistake one for a luxury watch, but Honor seems to want to flip all that on its head with the Honor Watch 6 Pro. Just take a look at this excerpt from the company’s press release:

Framing this brilliant display is a Precision Nanocrystalline Ceramic bezel crafted through a 1400°C high-temperature integrated sintering process, delivering a delicate texture and rich visual depth.

Inspired by modernist architecture, its diamond-cut octagonal rotatable crown features a dual-layer horizontal and vertical cut, complemented by a side button for intuitive navigation.

That sounds pretty wild to me, and it’s backed up by specs that sound just as legit, too. On top is a 1.5-inch LTPO Tandem AMOLED display that outputs 5,500-nit peak brightness. Underneath is a bevy of new sensors called the Intelligent Sense System, which Honor says combines “multi-wavelength monitoring, multi-optical path patrol technology, and anti-interference glass technology to reach deep skin layers.”

Runners will be pleased to know that the Honor Watch 6 Pro not only incorporates a dual-band GPS, but combines it with a “star-pointing 3-antenna design” and an AI fusion algorithm, which should deliver ultra-accurate tracking in any location and no matter how you hold your wrist. Despite its luxury looks, this one tracks over 120 sports modes, including Honor’s Professional Running Coach feature, which tracks running posture, lactate threshold, heart rate targets, and keeps you on pace with real-time voice coaching.

During sports modes like Badminton, the watch can identify more than six types of swings, and Honor says other sports modes can deliver similarly detailed statistics. The watch is 5ATM, IP68, IP69, and IP69K water-and-dust-resistant rated, plus it integrates directly with Strava so you can pull all your fitness data together.

Honor is incorporating a new wrist twist gesture system this time around, letting you use gestures to perform actions hands-free. The watch is rated at 10 days of battery life on a single charge in regular usage mode, while endurance mode will stretch that to 18 days. Pre-sales begin today, November 19, and run until December 31, when the watch is expected to launch, starting at €279/£229 for early bird pricing. After launch, the retail MSRP starts at €379/£329.

A teaser points to a Dec. 29 reveal for a card that promises four times the S80’s 16GB of memory.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Moore Threads’ MTT S80 product manager says that the next Lushan gaming GPU will have more VRAM than any consumer graphics card currently available, VideoCardz reports. With Nvidia’s RTX 5090 already at 32GB, Lushan would need more than that. This matches the previous promise of four times the company’s MTT S80’s 16GB, putting the flagship Lushan at 64GB.

Moore Threads apparently hasn’t given up on graphics, even four years after the MTT S80’s release. The company teased the card earlier this week with a poster for MDC 2026, its developer conference, showing a graphics card under a black cloth on a mountainside, and put Lushan’s name in the title of the post. Not disclosed; “4x” the S80 claimed, up to 64GB by our math

$1,999 MSRP; from $6,599 at Newegg (Nov. 15)

2,999 yuan with a motherboard (about $410) at launch; 1,499 yuan (about $225) at JD.com, Nov. 21

The Lushan would be a graphics rendering chip based on the Huagang architecture, with a sibling Huashan for AI. Performance claims are lofty at 15x for AAA gaming, 50x ray tracing, 64x AI compute, and 16x geometry processing over the MTT S80, based on information from MDC 2025, last year’s edition of the conference. To get there, Lushan adds a new hardware ray-tracing engine, the UniTE rendering architecture, AI generative rendering, which MyDrivers describes as the manufacturer’s answer to Nvidia’s DLSS and AMD’s FSR for upscaling and multi-frame generation, and DirectX 12 Ultimate support.This is a mighty high goal, as the previous outing, with the S80, fell short of expectations. Released in November 2022 at 2,999 yuan (about $410), the S80 was at first sold only in a bundle with an Asus motherboard. It now costs about half that in China, despite rising GPU costs. The 16GB GDDR6, PCIe 5.0 GPU, which pulls 255W, only matched a low-end card when tested on immature drivers in early 2023; it struggled against a GT 1030.

Moore Threads said last December that it had shipped monthly driver updates since and claimed the S80 then benchmarked 3.4x higher than at launch. The new 15x target is more reasonable when put against the backdrop of just how far the S80 trailed when it landed.

If we look beyond the consumer space, there are plenty of cards that have more than 32GB, and 64GB for that matter. The Nvidia RTX Pro 6000 Blackwell workstation card, for instance, has 96GB of GDDR7. It’s also possible to get RTX 4090s rebuilt with 48GB, at least in some Chinese shops, and a listing in September offered an RTX 5090 modded to 96GB. Maxsun also has the Arc Pro B60 Dual, built on Intel GPUs, with 2x 24GB on just one board. However, memory supply remains tight, with crushing memory shortages, and is projected to remain so, while GDDR7 remains quite costly.

Domestic rival Lisuan’s G100, meanwhile, is only at 12GB and 24GB for consumer and enterprise. A 64GB Lushan would leave those in the dust, which may hint that Moore Threads is targeting Western cards rather than just domestic rivals. It also raises the question of just how much memory you need for certain workloads.

#10366. AI agents design an entirely new CPU architecture from scratch, startup claims

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get started with free access to reviews, badges and discussions.

Go beyond the headlines with expert reporting on the hardware industry.

Track upcoming CPUs, GPUs and tech releases before they arrive.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Its lead shrinks to 3.83x on the 15 workloads without AI tuning.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Architect Labs, a Palo Alto startup, said in a research blog post that its AI systems discovered, designed, and verified Frostbite, a general-purpose CPU with a new instruction set “built from the ground up.” Across 25 workloads, a four-tile Frostbite cluster delivered 4.59 times the performance per watt of a similarly sized “silicon-proven” dual-issue, in-order CPU core. Those numbers come from a simulated 7nm design that has not yet been made in silicon, and the company says it can’t name the comparison core for legal reasons.

Conventional CPUs have hardware that “rediscovers, at runtime, which instructions actually depend on each other.” Multicore designs also need deep cache hierarchies and coherence protocols, which keep every core’s view of shared memory consistent, but scale poorly and cost area and power. Architect Labs’ alternative is what it calls “multithreading for free,” where the program itself exposes the parallel work, without threads, locks, or out-of-order hardware required for that discovery.

Frostbite’s programs are compiled to interaction nets, a model of computation from 1990 in which a program is a graph of nodes and computation happens by rewriting pairs of connected nodes, and the chip is designed around this distinct paradigm. Pairs of nodes being rewritten never overlap, so they can run in any order, or all at once, and arrive at the same result. Every value is also used exactly once, so data can stay in small local memories instead of large shared caches.

An example of the difference given by the team is a 5x5 chess-like board with five queens, placed so none can attack another. A typical program searches for solutions one board at a time. As an interaction net, the same search breaks into 3,183 small operations, and the longest chain that has to run in order is only 262 steps, so on average about 12 operations are ready to run at once at each step. Nothing about the program was rewritten to get there, as the parallel work was already intrinsic.

Each of the chip’s tiles, small identical processing units linked by an on-chip network, has its own small local memory. Four tiles, plus four L3 nodes, form the cluster that’s benchmarked against the conventional core for the performance claims, but the full layout has 16 tiles. The design reached 800 MHz in early synthesis but ended up at 500 MHz in the final layout. The cluster also averaged 8.77 times the comparison core’s performance per area, and the table below shows how its performance per watt varies by workload.

Frostbite performance per watt vs. comparison core

It may come as some surprise that the AI which built Frostbite started with what the company calls “zero architectural priors,” though the team first picked the workloads it had to do well on, and this sort of blank slatism may have its advantages in avoiding bias. Instead, the agents read papers and benchmarks, built Python models, and then selected one concept for focus. The original 141-page PDF output, which contained a programming model, runtime, and high-level blocks, was deleted after the first register-transfer level (RTL) design was generated, because “many of the numbers and estimates were well off what was physically possible.”

That PDF was only ever a starting point. Once the agents picked an architecture, they moved away from English — as it can’t get every detail right or catch every mistake, according to the team — and wrote the spec in the company’s own language, called ASL. ASL translates to Lean, a proof checker, so it can be machine-checked, which the company says stops a large class of bugs and cuts down on agents gaming their objectives. From this, the agents devised Arcmodel, a software model of the whole chip written in ASL, before any circuit design existed.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Simulating the RTL is slow, so the agents tested ideas on the model instead, which ran about 200 times faster. At first, the model’s power estimates were off by up to almost 250%, but an automated loop adjusted it until it matched the circuit design within just 5%.

From there, swarms of agents proposed changes in parallel, and each one was checked against the spec, tried in the fast model, built into the circuit design, and measured for power before being kept or dropped. This led to 58 ideas tried, with 37 kept. Separately, a behavior emerged that “was never specified by any human,” in what is known as work stealing: a tile with nothing to do can pull spare work from a neighbor or, in lieu of anything to do, go idle to save power.

Share of energy (one four-tile cluster, mean of 20 workloads, excluding clock distribution)

Fetching, decoding, and issuing instructions

Clock distribution (share of total energy, left out of the rows above)

This isn’t Architect Labs’ first design, as it showed off Redwood, an AI inference accelerator for robots and drones, in August. In that case, the company says its AI generated the RTL, verification, firmware, drivers, and kernels in under two weeks from a spec written by two human architects. Redwood still runs only on an FPGA and hasn’t been made in silicon, though the company says it is working toward having it built at TSMC.

Tendrils Compute, a possible rival based in Cambridge, U.K., raised funding earlier this month for a processor it says will have “thousands of cores operating in parallel.” It is also developing Vine, the same language Frostbite’s programs are written in.

Architect Labs says more benchmarks are coming, but they’re only part of the plan. It also wants an FPGA-hosted “software playground for anybody to run a program on Frostbite” along with a “preliminary operating system.” Eventually, it plans to release Frostbite’s RTL and instruction set.

In the meantime, the company plans to cover more workloads and compare “a broader set of CPUs,” including larger Frostbite mesh configurations against multicore versions of the comparison core with large caches. It also plans to test against “larger, wider, out-of-order CPUs,” with these comparisons due in the coming months on popular CPU benchmark suites such as SPEC and PARSEC.

#10364. How to Test WCAG 2.2 in CI: What axe-core Misses

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Your accessibility check in CI is green. axe-core ran, zero violations, and the badge in the README looks great. Your site can still fail WCAG 2.2.

That isn't a bug in axe. WCAG 2.2 became a W3C Recommendation in October 2023. It added nine success criteria and dropped one (4.1.1 Parsing), and most of the new ones can't be judged by reading the DOM. Deque said as much when it shipped the first WCAG 2.2 support in axe-core 4.5: the target-size rule would probably be the only new-criterion rule they added, because the others can't be automated without a lot of false positives.

So the rest is on you. This article covers the one criterion axe can check and three it can't (focus not obscured, dragging movements, accessible authentication), with Playwright tests you can drop into a project. I ran all of them against a small test page that fails every criterion on purpose, and against a fixed version of the same page. If you want a plain-language rundown of every change first, WCAG 2.2 vs 2.1 goes through them. Here I only care about what you can put in a pipeline.

Level A and AA are what most contracts and laws point at, so those are the ones that matter first. The AAA criteria are still worth knowing about.

That's four AA criteria with something you can script, one of which axe already handles. The rest of this article goes through those four.

The AA rule is smaller than most people expect: a pointer target should be at least 24 by 24 CSS pixels, or sit far enough from its neighbours that a 24px circle centred on it wouldn't overlap another target or that target's own circle. There are exceptions, including inline links in a sentence, and targets whose size the browser controls.

axe-core has a target-size rule for this. One catch: it isn't part of a default run. In my setup (axe-core 4.14.1 through @axe-core/playwright 4.14.0), a plain analyze() on the broken page reported nothing at all. The rule only showed up once I asked for it:

On the broken page this lists the three 16px toolbar buttons by selector. On the fixed page, with 32px buttons, it passes. If you've been running axe for years and never seen a target-size result, this is why.

Some context on how common the problem is. In the scan data behind my last SitePoint article, 34.5% of the 1,025 sites had controls below a stricter 44px threshold. That is a tougher standard than the 24px that AA asks for, so don't read it as "a third of sites fail 2.5.8". Plenty of those would pass. But it does show how many production sites ship tiny controls.

If you'd like a quick look at one page before wiring anything up, browser tools such as axe DevTools, WAVE, or a WCAG checker will show the same thing.

axe can't test it, because you have to press Tab and look at where the browser scrolled. Two details caught me out building this:

The check below samples a grid of points inside the visible part of the focused element and asks the browser what's on top at each one. If none of the points land on the element or its children, it's covered.

On my broken page it reported nine hits: links hidden behind the footer going forward ("Section 4 link", "Section 10 link", "Section 16 link") and the toolbar buttons and a few links hidden behind the header coming back. The output names the element and the key, which is what you want when you're staring at a failed build.

The fix is one CSS rule, sized to your sticky elements:

That tells the browser to leave room when it scrolls a focused element into view. With it in place the same test passes.

Be aware of what this can't see: a modal that opens over the page, or a chat widget that only appears after a delay, will not show up unless your test opens or waits for them. Adrian Roselli's piece on 2.4.11 and adversarial conformance is worth reading for the awkward cases.

Anything you can do by dragging needs a way to do it with a single click or tap, unless dragging is essential. Sortable lists, sliders, kanban cards, file reorderers: all of them.

You can't prove a drag alternative works from a script. But you can catch the obvious mistake, which is a draggable item with no button anywhere near it:

The limit is real. It only recognises the native draggable attribute. Libraries built on pointer events, such as dnd-kit, don't set it, so this test will walk right past them. If you use one of those, replace the selector with whatever your component renders, or add a test per drag component. Treat it as a tripwire for the common case, not a verdict.

The fix, in the fixed page, is a pair of "Move up" and "Move down" buttons on each item, each with an accessible name that says which item it moves.

You can't make someone solve a cognitive test to log in (remember a password, transcribe a code) unless there's an alternative or a mechanism that helps. Password managers count as that mechanism, which means the two ways sites break it are blocking paste and omitting the right autocomplete values.

This dispatches a synthetic paste event and checks whether anything called preventDefault() on it. It catches the old onpaste="return false" trick, and it catches script-based blockers too. It does not tell you whether your login has a CAPTCHA that needs an alternative, or an emailed one-time code you must retype. Those need a person.

On a pull request, a plain GitHub Actions job is enough:

Point PAGE_URL at a running preview, and run the tests against the pages that matter most: sign-in, checkout, anything with a sticky element. Don't try to crawl everything on the first pass. A test that fails for reasons nobody owns gets deleted within a month.

Four things I haven't tried to automate, and wouldn't:

Automation will keep the regressions out. It won't tell you the work is finished. A green build and a conformant site are different things, and WCAG 2.2 widened the gap between them.

The tests and both test pages are small enough to read in one sitting. Copy what's useful, change the selectors, and keep the broken page around: it's a handy way to check that a test fails when it should.

About the author: [name] builds WCAGsafe, an accessibility scanner. The study data above comes from its scans; the code in this article is independent of it and runs against any page.

#10360. A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

We’ve collaborated with partners who have built A2UI 1.0 demos across different industries to give you inspiration on how to adapt A2UI to your use cases. Explore what they’ve built:

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

Agent Anomaly Detection: Safeguarding agents across other clouds

Chips designers have to prove that their semiconductors do not break U.S. export control rules.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The U.S. Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) has released an interim final rule (IFR) [PDF] that will prevent Chinese AI companies from leasing data compute outside of China to access advanced U.S. chips like Nvidia Blackwell AI GPUs. However, Nikkei Asia reports that the new regulations have also affected foundries making chips for consumers and the automotive industry, as TSMC has put several shipments to both Chinese and non-Chinese chip designers on hold as it reviews their compliance with the changes to U.S.

Washington’s previous export controls only focused on high-performance AI chips like Nvidia’s Blackwell and upcoming Vera Rubin chips. The new rules expand it to include all chips, with front-end fabricators (chip foundries) or OSATs (outsourced semiconductor assembly and test companies), also known as packaging and testing companies, required to assume that semiconductors with a 16nm or 14nm node are automatically classified as export-controlled advanced computing chips. The ruling even mentioned that even if the designed chip has less than 30 billion transistors and has no HBM, it cannot be assumed that the chips aren’t built for high-performance computing.

The 16nm and 14nm nodes have been around for around 10 years now, and they could even be found inside consumer devices like smartphones and TVs, as well as in automotive applications. This new rule means that designers making chips using 16nm or newer nodes, even if they’re not specifically built for data centers, must secure documentation from an approved designer certifying the chip’s Total Processing Performance or performance density if they want to avoid additional scrutiny from the fab.

"The new rule is very strict this time, and the affecting scope is far beyond only chips for data centers but covers smartphone processors, autonomous driving and other types of chips for consumer electronics as well as others if they are using nodes that are 14nm or 16nm or more advanced tech," a source told the publication. "The move would delay shipments to many chip developers worldwide before they can clear compliance and hit many emerging startups in many Asian nations."

Aside from this, the U.S. also now requires export licenses to ship advanced AI chips to the following countries: Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Brazil, and Thailand. The IFR says that these countries are often used as transshipment points for chips that are being diverted to China, as evidenced by the Supermicro and Megaspeed investigations, which used one of these countries as the destination of the Nvidia servers they ordered, before sending them forward to their final destination in China.

Chip smuggling has long been a simmering issue in the United States, but it seemingly has bubbled over after the revelation of Jensen Huang’s affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, who’s also suspected of being instrumental in diverting $7.5 billion worth of Nvidia chips and passing on information to Chinese intelligence. While his alleged relationship with a direct subordinate was the apparent primary cause of his departure from Nvidia, sources say that the real reason behind his ousting is the mass smuggling of 1.5 million Nvidia AI GPUs that happened right under his nose.

That’s why lots of people have started moving to personal NAS (network-attached storage) units in their home, which don’t require any subscription to use and are safe on your home network (instead of in some random data center). Plus, they’re up to $160 off right now.

Today’s best value is the 2-bay UGREEN DXP2800 GT, which is $349.99 right now ($160 off). It’s ideal for saving a ton of movies, security camera footage, lots of photos and files from your PCs and smartphones, and even running entire apps. You can set up automatic backup of all your data (including your phone’s) with the UGREEN NAS app, which means you can finally ditch that ridiculous cloud storage plan and keep it all local.

If you don’t need that much power and just need data backup, the beginner-friendly UGREEN DH2300 is a 2-bay NAS on sale for $199.99 ($70 off), making it much more affordable to get started. This holds up to two 32TB drives, so you can expand your storage to nearly ludicrous levels.

Power users will want to consider the UGREEN DXP4800 GT for $527.99 ($132 off). This one sports a Ryzen chipset inside, support for four drives (up to 144TB of storage), and even has dual Ethernet ports for ultimate performance.

If you’re new to NAS units, know that you’ll also need to buy drives to fit into them. NAS units are essentially computers that are designed to easily store a bunch of your stuff, but most of them are sold without drives inside. To get started, I recommend buying these 4TB Seagate IronWolf NAS hard drives, which are on sale for $189. If that’s not enough storage, Western Digital has an 8TB Red Plus NAS HDD that’s down to $339.99, a $40 discount from the usual price. Just remember to click the coupon on the Amazon page to get the best deal.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The U.S. delivered more than 500 RQ-20A Puma AE drones to Ukraine in 2023 for use in reconnaissance missions. These hand-launched uncrewed aerial vehicles (UAVs) are equipped with optical and infrared sensors for surveying the battlefield, but the evolving electromagnetic warfare landscape meant that the entire fleet had to be grounded. Now, Ukrainian defense firm Rise Technologies has upgraded the drones with its Palych communications system, which allows the UAVs to withstand the advanced electronic warfare (EW) environment found on the frontlines in 2026. According to United24 Media, this new component allows drone operators to select the drone’s operating frequency band to avoid heavily jammed channels and maintain communications during flight. It also increases the range of the Puma to 31 miles (50 kilometers) from its basic range of 10 miles (16 kilometers).

Russia’s 2022 escalation of the war in Ukraine saw the widespread use of small and medium-sized drones, with both sides deploying countermeasures like GPS jamming and radio interference to defend against these UAVs. Because of this, we’ve seen various innovations to counter the countermeasures, like the use of fiber optic drones, which are essentially jam-proof, and the installation of a camera and magnetic compass on Russian drones to counter GPS jamming. Ukraine has also devised a "flying modem" relay system for its drones, allowing its forces to conduct strategic drone strikes even without the use of finicky Starlink connections. The indigenous communications system was also installed on other drones from DJI and Autel, as well as on some attack UAVs, giving Ukrainian drone operators an advantage on the battlefield.

The proliferation of drones has also forced militaries across the world to develop cheaper countermeasures against them. The U.S. has already deployed high-energy lasers to shoot down Mexican cartel drones crossing its southern border, while the Royal Navy is testing the DragonFire laser that can target UAVs traveling at up to 400 mph while only costing $13 per shot. Israel has also deployed its 100,000-watt Iron Beam system, while China has built a man-portable anti-drone laser system that weighs 55 pounds and can hit targets up to 1,600 feet away.

These laser systems are ideal for taking down a few drone units. But for drone swarm threats, microwave systems are a better solution, as they could take down multiple drones in a single shot. Some examples of this include the Leonidas AGV from the U.S. and Turkey’s Aselsan Ejderha, or “Dragon” in English, which uses high-power electromagnetic systems to overwhelm the sensitive electronics found inside most drones.

#10350. Google’s 3D emoji has wild Android spec requirements: 10GB of free space, 6GB RAM

Google introduced 3D emoji on Pixel this year, and has just detailed the minimum specifications required to get them on your Android phone.

Update: Google replied on Reddit to “clear up some confusion” about the Help Center page. The listed storage and RAM requirements refer to the “automatic background download via Google Play services on eligible Android 17 devices that didn’t ship with the 3D font preinstalled.” The support article did not make that distinction.

Noto 3D emoji, with its high-resolution bitmap approach, uses less than 220 MB of storage versus the previous 2D vector font that comes in at around 3 MB.

During installation, Google Play services temporarily needs a bit of extra headroom to copy the file into place before immediately clearing its download cache.

Meanwhile, Google said RAM usage is “minimal & transient” as “Android doesn’t load the 3D emoji font file into RAM.” The support page listed 6 GB as a “cutoff to ensure lower-memory devices stay on the lightweight ~3 MB 2D vector font.”

Noto 3D emoji goes from vector-based 2D emoji to a high-resolution bitmap font. This results in “high-resolution designs” for emoji “with more texture and dimension to characters and icons.” Google has an FAQ section about why the new emoji might look low resolution:

Vector vs. bitmap rendering: Older Android emoji used 2D vector graphics, which scale infinitely to any size without losing resolution. The 3D emoji are bitmap graphics, which are pre-rendered pixel-based images. When these images are scaled up by an app or zoomed in, the pixels stretch, which can cause a softer or lower-resolution appearance.

The Google support article published this week lists the minimum specs your Android device must have to get 3D emoji:

The last two bullet points are rather surprising. In fact, Google even lists instructions for checking how much memory and storage your device has:

In terms of availability, Google says a supported device will “automatically download the 3D emoji when it’s on Wi-Fi and charging.”

At this time, there isn’t a way to force download the emoji.

Google announced 3D emoji in May, with the new design shipping on the Pixel 11 in August. It has since arrived on older Pixel devices.

In terms of OEM availability, Google explicitly says “Samsung devices use a proprietary emoji font and do not receive the 3D font update.” Additionally:

This is also true for third-party messaging apps, such as WhatsApp, who use their own emoji fonts rather than relying on your device’s system font.

‘You immediately get your portal to the world back.’

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elon Musk’s Neuralink started clinical trials less than three years ago, and patients are seeing impressive results. The latest demonstration of this brain computer interface comes via a video on social media showing an unnamed medical trial participant playing GTA 6. According to the video, the team behind the implant mapped different i,agined movements to a PlayStation 5 controller, allowing the player to simulate controller actions using his thoughts. For example, thinking about tapping their ring finger activated the square button while imagining sliding their right hand forward pulled the nose of the airplane up. They also mentioned another control being bound to their foot, it’s unclear what action this allowed.

Playing GTA VI with a Neuralink https://t.co/ViFjXK9OSVNovember 20, 2026

The first Neuralink device was implanted in January 2024, with the patient able to move a mouse just by thinking. Once they’ve been able to master the basics of using a computer, like moving a mouse and using the keyboard, through the brain-computer interface (BCI), one of the next steps that some patients take is to play a game on their system. Just a couple of months after the first implant, we’ve already seen reports of people using Neuralink to play turn-based games like chess and Civilization VI. By August 2024, the second Neuralink recipient was reportedly playing Counter-Strike 2, with the patient calling the experience “insane.”

Today, we already have people playing complicated games like World of Warcraft and GTA VI using BCI implants. This is an achievement for Neuralink and a life-changing capability for patients who have lost mobility. The patient who streamed their GTA VI gameplay said that they’ve previously used a mouth stick, but it was miles away from the Neuralink chip they have in their head. “Before the … chip and surgery, I was using a mouth stick and an iPad. I was essentially like a bird just pecking at an iPad. There was (sic) like a few games I could play, but like obviously it made your jaw sore, it made everything sore,” the patient said. “So, then I get a chip, and I was like what does this let me do. Immediately, controlling a computer is like boom, ‘portal to the world,’ like you just immediately get your portal to the world back.”

Aside from giving people the ability to control computing devices with their mind, Neuralink is also working on a technology, called Blindsight, that will restore sight to blind people. Musk claims that it’s already “ready from an engineering standpoint,” and that the company is only waiting for regulatory approval before it can begin human testing.

#10341. How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

AI agents now read email, book travel and move money for the people who use them. Every tool call also pulls in text that someone else wrote, and whoever controls that text can try to steer the agent. OWASP calls this agent goal hijack and ranks it first in its Top 10 for Agentic Applications (2026).

In September we announced Agent Anomaly Detection in Private Preview on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform. It reads your agents' traces and logs asynchronously, without adding latency, and flags sessions where an agent acts outside its intended boundaries. We tested this feature against benchmarks for this top risk of agent goal hijack.

Security tools tend to look good on tests their own team wrote, so we ran five non-Google-written, public agent-security benchmarks through Agent Anomaly Detection without changing anything. In four of them, the harmful instruction hides in data that a tool returns. In the fifth, the user asks for harm directly.

The model itself didn't follow about half of the harmful instructions it read. It refused three in four direct requests for harm, and ignored or failed at about 30% of the instructions hidden in tool output. Of the ones that were executed, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% for post-execution review. It raised false alarms on 2% to 16% of benign sessions, depending on the tools the agent uses. Poisoned memory is the clear gap: when instructions planted in the agent’s long-term memory made it act, Agent Anomaly Detection caught 4 of 18.

We ran each benchmark case through an agent built with the Agent Development Kit (ADK) using gemini-2.5-flash, on the benchmark's simulated apps. The agent sent its traces and logs to Agent Anomaly Detection, as a customer's agent would. Then the benchmark's own Python check read the final state of the apps, such as whether money left the account, and labeled the session. Deterministic code decides the label, not a model, so an injected instruction can't mislead the grader, and Agent Anomaly Detection cannot cheat the benchmark. Each agent first ran at least 300 benign sessions, because Agent Anomaly Detection learns how each agent usually behaves.

We also recorded whether a tool returned the harmful instruction to the agent. In many cases, the agent never opens the email or file that holds it. So each harmful session ends one of three ways:

In AgentHarm, the user sends the request, so the agent always reads it.

The five benchmarks are AgentDojo (NeurIPS 2024), InjecAgent (ACL 2024 Findings), Agent Security Bench (ASB, ICLR 2025), AgentDyn (2026) and AgentHarm (ICLR 2025). ASB plants the instruction in the agent's long-term memory. In all, Agent Anomaly Detection scored 1,774 sessions.

Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% of the harmful instructions that the agent carried out, and raised a few false alarms on ordinary office work. It's strongest on goal hijack and tool misuse, and weakest on poisoned memory.

Each finding carries a threat category, and four of Agent Anomaly Detection’s five categories align with the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. We graded each OWASP category on what this study showed, not on what Agent Anomaly Detection is designed to catch.

Public benchmarks concentrate on goal hijack and tool misuse, so our evidence is strongest there. We did not find public benchmarks with labeled agent traces for cascading failures or for identity and privilege abuse. Shared benchmarks for those categories would help the whole field.

By benchmark, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 85% of the carried-out harmful instructions on AgentDojo, 87% on InjecAgent, 99% on AgentDyn and 81% on AgentHarm. Over all harmful sessions, including the ones that the agent never read or resisted, it flagged 45% to 62%. In the 141 sessions where the agent never read the instruction, nothing happened, and it flagged just 1.

Results held across the apps. In 8 of the 11 apps, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged at least 95% of the carried-out harmful instructions. The lowest rate was in AgentDojo's Slack app, at 63%. Many of those instructions ask for one small action, such as a page visit, which looks like normal browsing.

Agent Anomaly Detection also flags many requests that the agent refuses. In AgentHarm, the agent refused most requests and carried out 108 of 412, and Agent Anomaly Detection still flagged 55% of the refused sessions, because the request sits in the user's own message, which it reads. When the agent didn't follow an instruction hidden in tool output, it flagged only 11% to 25%, because the instruction sits deep inside a tool result.

False alarms were rare on ordinary office work: on the three injection benchmarks, whose benign sessions are office tasks, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 2% to 4% of benign sessions. On AgentHarm it flagged 16%. Those benign tasks are twins of the harmful ones, so they post to social media, run terminal commands and download files. High-impact tools in normal use look more like misuse.

Agent Anomaly Detection did less well in five areas. It flagged only 11% to 25% of the instructions that the agent resisted, because a resisted instruction changes little in how the agent behaves. It missed more small actions, like single page visits, because they look like normal work. And it raised more false alarms for agents whose everyday work uses high-impact tools. If your agent is one of them, expect more findings at first, and triage them by severity and threat category.

The other two areas are new agents and poisoned memory. Some Agent Anomaly Detection methods need a few hundred sessions of history: on AgentDyn, it flagged 33% of carried-out harmful instructions when each agent had about 220 benign sessions, and 99% with 360. And it caught only 4 of the 18 sessions where poisoned memory made the agent act.

Protect your agents with defense in depth. Use controls that stop harmful actions before they run, and Agent Anomaly Detection to find what gets through.

We used one agent model and simulated apps, and the benchmarks' benign tasks are likely simpler than your production traffic, so your false-alarm rate can differ. Agent Anomaly Detection returned no result for about 6% of the sessions we sent; counting those as misses lowers the overall rates by 1 to 2 points.

Agent Anomaly Detection is in Private Preview. To request access, read the Agent Anomaly Detection overview.

Agent Anomaly Detection: Safeguarding agents across other clouds

Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

Did the user ask for this? Securing AI agents against intent hijacking with Semantic governance policies
